Matthew cross-references

1022 completed source verses; 1055 retained pairs; 1068 source units read; 46 deferred units.

First unfinished: Matthew 1:14. Next new source review in this book: none. All original source units in this book have been read. Overall next new source: Luke 17:1.

Completed means an initial curated cross-reference pass: the complete supplied English source unit and each retained target passage were read, and each pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean exhaustive coverage or independent specialist approval.

Matthew 1:1

A book of the origin of Jesus Christ, son of David, son of Abraham.

  1. Romans 1:3 genealogy

    Matthew introduces Jesus Christ as David's son; Romans likewise identifies God's Son as coming from David's seed according to the flesh. The shared claim concerns Davidic descent.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 1:3 concerning His Son, who came from David’s seed according to the flesh,

    Pinned target record

  2. Galatians 3:16 promise and fulfillment

    Matthew's title calls Christ Abraham's son. Galatians interprets the promises to Abraham's seed as concerning Christ, providing an explicit apostolic connection between Abraham's lineage and Christ.

    Read matching passage

    Galatians 3:16 Now the promises were spoken to Abraham and to his seed. It does not say, “And to seeds,” as concerning many, but as concerning one, “And to your seed,” who is Christ.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:2

Abraham begot Isaac; and Isaac begot Jacob; and Jacob begot Judah and his brothers;

  1. Genesis 25:19 genealogy

    Genesis explicitly says that Abraham begot Isaac, the first father-son relationship in Matthew's verse.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 25:19 And these are the generations of Isaac, Abraham’s son: Abraham begot Isaac.

    Pinned target record

  2. Genesis 25:26 genealogy

    Genesis records Jacob's birth to Isaac and Rebekah, supplying the narrative behind Matthew's statement that Isaac begot Jacob.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 25:26 And afterward his brother came out, and his hand held Esau’s heel; and he was named Jacob. And Isaac was sixty years old when Rebekah bore them.

    Pinned target record

  3. Genesis 29:35 genealogy

    Genesis describes Leah bearing Judah to Jacob, supplying the birth narrative for Matthew's Jacob-Judah link.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 29:35 And she conceived again and bore a son, and said, “This time I will give thanks to the Lord.” Therefore she called his name Judah; and she ceased bearing.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:3

Judah begot Perez and Zerah by Tamar; Perez begot Hezron; Hezron begot Ram;

  1. Genesis 38:27–38:30 genealogy

    Genesis recounts the twins Perez and Zerah and the scarlet-thread incident. In the surrounding Tamar narrative this is the birth episode Matthew compresses into his identification of Judah's sons by Tamar.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 38:27 And when she gave birth, behold, there were twins in her womb.

    Genesis 38:28 And as she was giving birth, one put out his hand; and the midwife took it and bound a scarlet thread upon his hand, saying, “This one shall come out first.”

    Genesis 38:29 But as he drew back his hand, immediately his brother came out; and she said, “Why has a barrier been broken through because of you?” And his name was called Perez.

    Genesis 38:30 And afterward his brother came out, upon whose hand was the scarlet thread; and his name was called Zerah.

    Pinned target record

  2. Ruth 4:18–4:19 genealogy

    Ruth's closing genealogy follows Perez to Hezron and then Arran; Matthew follows Perez to Hezron and Ram. The sequences are comparable while the edition's different name forms remain visible.

    Read matching passage

    Ruth 4:18 Now these are the generations of Perez: Perez begot Hezron,

    Ruth 4:19 and Hezron begot Arran, and Arran begot Amminadab,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:4

Ram begot Amminadab; Amminadab begot Nahshon; Nahshon begot Salmon;

  1. Ruth 4:19–4:20 genealogy

    Ruth follows Arran through Amminadab and Nahshon to Salman; Matthew gives Ram, Amminadab, Nahshon and Salmon. The overlapping succession supports comparison without silently replacing the Saints edition's name forms.

    Read matching passage

    Ruth 4:19 and Hezron begot Arran, and Arran begot Amminadab,

    Ruth 4:20 and Amminadab begot Nahshon, and Nahshon begot Salman,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:5

Salmon begot Boaz by Rahab; Boaz begot Obed by Ruth; Obed begot Jesse;

  1. Ruth 4:13–4:17 genealogy

    Ruth narrates Boaz taking Ruth as wife and her bearing the child subsequently named Obed, whose descendants are Jesse and David. Matthew compresses those relationships into the genealogy; Ruth does not identify Rahab as Boaz's mother.

    Read matching passage

    Ruth 4:13 Boaz took Ruth, and she became his wife. He went in to her, and the Lord gave her conception, and she bore a son.

    Ruth 4:14 The women said to Naomi, “Blessed be the Lord, who has not left you without the near kinsman today; and may he call your name in Israel.

    Ruth 4:15 He shall be a restorer of life to you and a nourisher of your old age, for your daughter-in-law who loved you has borne him, and she is better to you than seven sons.”

    Ruth 4:16 And Naomi took the child and laid him in her bosom, and became his nurse.

    Ruth 4:17 The neighbouring women gave him a name, saying, “A son is born to Naomi.” They called his name Obed. He was the father of Jesse, the father of David.

    Pinned target record

  2. Ruth 4:21–4:22 genealogy

    Both genealogies connect Salman/Salmon, Boaz, Obed and Jesse in succession. The Ruth passage also continues to David, but does not independently supply Matthew's statement about Rahab.

    Read matching passage

    Ruth 4:21 and Salman begot Boaz, and Boaz begot Obed,

    Ruth 4:22 and Obed begot Jesse, and Jesse begot David.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:6

Jesse begot David the king. David begot Solomon by the wife of Uriah;

  1. Ruth 4:22 genealogy

    Ruth's final verse explicitly says Jesse begot David, the first relationship stated in Matthew's verse.

    Read matching passage

    Ruth 4:22 and Obed begot Jesse, and Jesse begot David.

    Pinned target record

  2. 2 Samuel 12:24 genealogy

    Samuel narrates David and Bathsheba having Solomon, corresponding to Matthew's David-Solomon link. Matthew calls the mother the wife of Uriah, while this Samuel verse names her Bathsheba and calls her David's wife.

    Read matching passage

    2 Samuel 12:24 David comforted Bathsheba his wife. He went in to her and lay with her. She conceived, bore a son, and named him Solomon. The Lord loved him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:7

Solomon begot Rehoboam; Rehoboam begot Abijah; Abijah begot Asaph;

  1. 1 Chronicles 3:10 genealogy

    Chronicles gives Solomon, Rehoboam, Abia and Asa in the royal succession. Matthew has Solomon, Rehoboam, Abijah and Asaph: the overlapping sequence is clear, but the Asa/Asaph wording difference is retained and not treated as an exact name match.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 3:10 Solomon’s sons: Rehoboam; Abia his son, Asa his son, Jehoshaphat his son,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:8

Asaph begot Jehoshaphat; Jehoshaphat begot Joram; Joram begot Uzziah;

  1. 1 Chronicles 3:10–3:12 genealogy

    Chronicles supplies the succession Asa, Jehoshaphat, Joram, Ahaziah, Joash, Amasias, Azariah and Joathan. Compared with Matthew's Asaph, Jehoshaphat, Joram and Uzziah, it exposes both a name-form difference and Matthew's compressed generations; it does not make every Matthew link an immediate father-son relationship.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 3:10 Solomon’s sons: Rehoboam; Abia his son, Asa his son, Jehoshaphat his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:11 Joram his son, Ahaziah his son, Joash his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:12 Amasias his son, Azariah his son, Joathan his son,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:9

Uzziah begot Jotham; Jotham begot Ahaz; Ahaz begot Hezekiah;

  1. 1 Chronicles 3:12–3:13 genealogy

    Chronicles names Azariah, Joathan, Ahaz and Hezekiah, the royal succession corresponding to Matthew's Uzziah, Jotham, Ahaz and Hezekiah. The different name forms are shown rather than harmonized in the source text.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 3:12 Amasias his son, Azariah his son, Joathan his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:13 Ahaz his son, Hezekiah his son, Manasseh his son,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:10

Hezekiah begot Manasseh; Manasseh begot Amos; Amos begot Josiah;

  1. 1 Chronicles 3:13–3:14 genealogy

    Chronicles continues through Hezekiah, Manasseh, Amon and Josiah. Matthew gives Hezekiah, Manasseh, Amos and Josiah; the Amon/Amos difference remains explicit while the surrounding royal sequence agrees.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 3:13 Ahaz his son, Hezekiah his son, Manasseh his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:14 Amon his son, Josiah his son.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:11

Josiah begot Jechoniah and his brothers at the deportation to Babylon.

  1. 1 Chronicles 3:15–3:16 genealogy

    Chronicles places Joakim among Josiah's sons and Jechonias in Joakim's line. This is a useful comparison with Matthew's compressed Josiah-Jechoniah transition; Chronicles includes the intervening generation and does not corroborate an immediate father-son link between those two names.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 3:15 Josiah’s sons: Joanan the firstborn, Joakim the second, Sedekias the third, Salloum the fourth.

    1 Chronicles 3:16 Joakim’s sons: Jechonias his son, Sedekias his son.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:12

After the deportation to Babylon, Jechoniah begot Shealtiel; Shealtiel begot Zerubbabel;

  1. 1 Chronicles 3:17–3:19 genealogy

    The Saints Chronicles record places Salathiel among Jechonia-asir's sons and lists Zerubbabel among Salathiel's sons. This parallels Matthew's Jechoniah, Shealtiel and Zerubbabel sequence while retaining the edition's own spelling and parentage text.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 3:17 The sons of Jechonia-asir: Salathiel his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:18 Melchiram, Phadaias, Sanesar, Jekemia, Osamo, and Denethi.

    1 Chronicles 3:19 Salathiel’s sons: Zerubbabel and Semei. Zerubbabel’s sons: Mosollamos and Ananias, and Salomith their sister,

    Pinned target record

  2. Luke 3:27 genealogy

    Luke also places Zerubbabel next to Shealtiel, but continues to Neri rather than Matthew's Jechoniah. The link records the shared names and the differing ancestry instead of claiming that the two genealogies are identical.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 3:27 of Joanan, of Rhesa, of Zerubbabel, of Shealtiel, of Neri,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:13

Zerubbabel begot Abiud; Abiud begot Eliakim; Eliakim begot Azor;

  1. Luke 3:27 genealogy

    Both genealogies include Zerubbabel. Matthew follows him toward Abiud, Eliakim and Azor, whereas Luke's line toward Jesus goes through Rhesa and Joanan. This is a comparison at the shared ancestor, not independent confirmation of Matthew's three subsequent names.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 3:27 of Joanan, of Rhesa, of Zerubbabel, of Shealtiel, of Neri,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:16

and Jacob begot Joseph, the husband of Mary, of whom was born Jesus, who is called Christ.

  1. Luke 1:27 narrative parallel

    Both genealogies connect Jesus with Joseph while qualifying the connection: Matthew identifies Joseph as Mary's husband and says Jesus was born of Mary, while Luke says Jesus was Joseph's son as was supposed. Matthew calls Joseph's father Jacob while Luke names Heli; the difference is not resolved by this cross-reference.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 1:27 to a virgin betrothed to a man named Joseph, of David’s house. The virgin’s name was Mary.

    Pinned target record

  2. Luke 3:23 genealogy

    Both genealogies connect Jesus with Joseph while qualifying the connection: Matthew describes Joseph as Mary's husband, of whom Jesus was born, and Luke says Jesus was Joseph's son as was supposed. Matthew calls Joseph's father Jacob while Luke names Heli; the difference is not resolved by this cross-reference.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 3:23 Jesus Himself was about thirty years old when He began His ministry. He was the son, as was supposed, of Joseph, of Heli,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:17

So all the generations from Abraham to David were fourteen generations, from David to the deportation to Babylon fourteen generations, and from the deportation to Babylon to the Christ fourteen generations.

  1. 1 Chronicles 3:10–3:19 genealogy

    Chronicles' royal and exilic succession provides a comparison for Matthew's organized fourteen-generation summary. Its inclusion of Ahaziah, Joash, Amasias and Joakim makes Matthew's selectivity visible; this passage does not independently verify Matthew's three numerical groups of fourteen.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 3:10 Solomon’s sons: Rehoboam; Abia his son, Asa his son, Jehoshaphat his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:11 Joram his son, Ahaziah his son, Joash his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:12 Amasias his son, Azariah his son, Joathan his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:13 Ahaz his son, Hezekiah his son, Manasseh his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:14 Amon his son, Josiah his son.

    1 Chronicles 3:15 Josiah’s sons: Joanan the firstborn, Joakim the second, Sedekias the third, Salloum the fourth.

    1 Chronicles 3:16 Joakim’s sons: Jechonias his son, Sedekias his son.

    1 Chronicles 3:17 The sons of Jechonia-asir: Salathiel his son,

    1 Chronicles 3:18 Melchiram, Phadaias, Sanesar, Jekemia, Osamo, and Denethi.

    1 Chronicles 3:19 Salathiel’s sons: Zerubbabel and Semei. Zerubbabel’s sons: Mosollamos and Ananias, and Salomith their sister,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:18

The origin of Jesus Christ was like this. When His mother Mary was betrothed to Joseph, before they came together she was found to be with child from the Holy Spirit.

  1. Luke 1:34–1:35 narrative parallel

    Matthew attributes Mary's pregnancy before marital relations to the Holy Spirit. Luke has Mary ask how conception will occur without knowing a man and has the angel answer that the Holy Spirit will come upon her.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 1:34 Mary said to the angel, “How will this be, since I do not know a man?”

    Luke 1:35 The angel answered her, “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; and so the holy One to be born will be called Son of God.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:19

Joseph her husband, being righteous and unwilling to expose her to public disgrace, decided to divorce her quietly.

  1. Deuteronomy 24:1 thematic parallel

    Joseph's proposed divorce belongs to the legal world in which Deuteronomy describes a husband giving a wife a divorce certificate and sending her away. The law supplies divorce background; it does not narrate Joseph's case or prescribe his plan to act quietly.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 24:1 And if a man takes a wife and dwells with her as husband, and she finds no favour in his sight, because he has found some unseemly thing in her, and he writes her a certificate of divorce, and gives it into her hands, and sends her forth out of his house,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:20

As he considered these things, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to him in a dream and said, “Joseph, son of David, do not fear to take Mary your wife home, for what has been conceived in her is from the Holy Spirit.

  1. Luke 1:35 narrative parallel

    The angel's assurance to Joseph that Mary's conception is from the Holy Spirit corresponds to Gabriel's explanation to Mary that the Holy Spirit will come upon her. These are announcements to different recipients about the same conception, not the same reported angelic encounter.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 1:35 The angel answered her, “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; and so the holy One to be born will be called Son of God.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:21

She shall bear a Son, and you shall call His name Jesus, for He shall save His people from their sins.”

  1. Luke 1:31 narrative parallel

    Both angelic announcements direct that Mary's Son be named Jesus. Matthew addresses Joseph and explains the name through salvation from sins; Luke's instruction is addressed to Mary.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 1:31 And behold, you will conceive in your womb and bear a Son, and you shall name Him Jesus.

    Pinned target record

  2. Luke 1:76–1:77 thematic parallel

    Matthew explains Jesus' mission as saving His people from their sins. Zechariah's prophecy describes the child's preparation of the Lord's way as giving His people knowledge of salvation in the forgiveness of their sins; the shared connection is salvation and forgiveness, while the child addressed in Luke is John.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 1:76 And you also, child, will be called prophet of the Most High, for you will go before the Lord to prepare His ways,

    Luke 1:77 to give His people knowledge of salvation in the forgiveness of their sins,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:22

All this has happened so that what was spoken by the Lord through the prophet might be fulfilled:

  1. Isaiah 7:14 promise and fulfillment

    Matthew's fulfillment introduction leads directly into the following virgin-and-Emmanuel quotation. Isaiah supplies that identifiable oracle; the connection depends on reading Matthew's introduction together with its continuation in 1:23.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 7:14 Therefore the Lord Himself will give you a sign: behold, the virgin will conceive and bear a son, and you will call his name Emmanuel.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:23

“Behold, the virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and they shall call His name Emmanuel”; which means “God with us.”

  1. Isaiah 7:14 quotation

    Matthew quotes Isaiah's virgin conceiving and bearing a son named Emmanuel and adds the explanation 'God with us.' The Saints Isaiah text uses 'you will call his name' where Matthew has 'they shall call His name'; the supplied wording is preserved.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 7:14 Therefore the Lord Himself will give you a sign: behold, the virgin will conceive and bear a son, and you will call his name Emmanuel.

    Pinned target record

  2. Matthew 28:20 thematic parallel

    Within Matthew itself, the opening Emmanuel explanation, 'God with us,' corresponds to Jesus' closing assurance that He is with His disciples all the days. This is an internal literary and theological correspondence, not a second independent birth account.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 28:20 teaching them to keep all that I commanded you; and behold, I am with you all the days, until the consummation of the age.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:24

When Joseph awoke from sleep, he did as the angel of the Lord commanded him and took his wife home.

  1. Matthew 2:13–2:14 narrative parallel

    Matthew later repeats Joseph's response to an angelic dream: an instruction concerning the Child and His mother is followed by Joseph rising and carrying it out. This same-book narrative parallel connects Joseph's two acts of obedience without treating the dreams as one event.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 2:13 After they withdrew, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to Joseph in a dream, saying, “Rise, take the Child and His mother, and flee to Egypt. Stay there until I tell you, for Herod is about to seek the Child to destroy Him.”

    Matthew 2:14 He rose, took the Child and His mother by night, and withdrew to Egypt.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 1:25

He did not have relations with her until she bore a Son; and he called His name Jesus.

  1. Luke 2:21 narrative parallel

    Both accounts report the actual giving of the name Jesus in accordance with an angel's prior instruction. Luke supplies the naming at circumcision; it does not independently state Matthew's qualification about Joseph's relations with Mary.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 2:21 When eight days were completed for His circumcision, He was named Jesus, the name given by the angel before He was conceived in the womb.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:1

After Jesus was born in Bethlehem of Judea in the days of King Herod, behold, magi from the east arrived in Jerusalem,

  1. Luke 2:4–2:7 narrative parallel

    Luke locates Joseph and Mary in Bethlehem when Mary bears her Son, corresponding to Matthew's statement that Jesus was born in Bethlehem of Judea. Luke does not describe the magi in this passage.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 2:4 Joseph also went up from Galilee, from the city of Nazareth, into Judea, to David’s city, which is called Bethlehem, because he belonged to David’s house and family,

    Luke 2:5 to be registered with Mary, who was betrothed to him and was pregnant.

    Luke 2:6 While they were there, the time came for her to give birth.

    Luke 2:7 She bore her firstborn Son, wrapped Him in swaddling cloths, and laid Him in a manger, because there was no place for them in the lodging place.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:2

saying, “Where is the One born King of the Jews? For we saw His star at its rising and have come to bow before Him.”

  1. Numbers 24:17 allusion

    Balaam's oracle joins a rising star from Jacob with a ruler arising from Israel. Matthew's star and newborn King invite a royal-star comparison; Matthew does not explicitly quote the oracle, and Numbers does not describe the magi's astronomical observation.

    Read matching passage

    Numbers 24:17 I will show him, but not now; I pronounce him blessed, but he does not draw near. A star shall rise from Jacob, and a man shall arise from Israel. He shall crush Moab's leaders and plunder all the sons of Seth.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:3

When King Herod heard this, he was troubled, and all Jerusalem with him.

  1. Matthew 2:16 narrative parallel

    Within this same chapter, Herod's alarm at the report of a newborn king develops into his angry order to kill Bethlehem's boys. This is an internal narrative development of the same threat, not independent confirmation of Herod's reaction.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 2:16 Then Herod, seeing that the magi had outwitted him, became exceedingly angry. He sent men and killed all the boys in Bethlehem and throughout its district who were two years old and under, according to the exact time he had learned from the magi.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:4

He gathered all the chief priests and scribes of the people and asked them where the Christ was to be born.

  1. John 7:41–7:42 thematic parallel

    Herod asks the religious authorities where the Christ is to be born. John's crowd likewise debates the Christ's origin and appeals to Scripture for Davidic descent and Bethlehem; the passages share the specific scriptural birthplace question, not the same conversation.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:41 Others said, “This is the Christ.” But some said, “Surely the Christ does not come from Galilee?

    John 7:42 Has not the Scripture said that the Christ comes from the offspring of David and from Bethlehem, the village where David was?”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:5

They told him, “In Bethlehem of Judea, for this is what has been written through the prophet:

  1. Micah 5:1 quotation

    The authorities identify Bethlehem as the birthplace on the basis of a prophet. The following quotation is grounded in Micah's Bethlehem ruler oracle, numbered 5:1 in this Saints edition, rather than silently using another edition's 5:2.

    Read matching passage

    Micah 5:1 And you, Bethlehem, house of Ephrathah, are small to be among Judah’s thousands; from you one will come out for Me to be a ruler in Israel, and his goings out are from the beginning, from days of old.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:6

‘And you, Bethlehem, land of Judah, are by no means least among the rulers of Judah; for out of you will come a ruler who will shepherd My people Israel.’”

  1. Micah 5:1 quotation

    Both passages identify Bethlehem in Judah as the place from which Israel's ruler comes. Matthew's 'by no means least' reformulates Micah's description of Bethlehem as small, and Matthew's shepherd wording extends beyond this one Micah verse.

    Read matching passage

    Micah 5:1 And you, Bethlehem, house of Ephrathah, are small to be among Judah’s thousands; from you one will come out for Me to be a ruler in Israel, and his goings out are from the beginning, from days of old.

    Pinned target record

  2. 2 Samuel 5:2 allusion

    Matthew's ruler 'who will shepherd My people Israel' closely parallels the Lord's commission of David to shepherd His people Israel and be ruler over Israel in Samuel. It contributes the Davidic shepherd-ruler language alongside the Bethlehem oracle.

    Read matching passage

    2 Samuel 5:2 In days past, when Saul was king over us, you were the one who led Israel out and brought them in. The Lord said to you, ‘You will shepherd My people Israel, and you will be ruler over Israel.’”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:7

Then Herod secretly called the magi and learned from them the exact time when the star appeared.

  1. Matthew 2:16 narrative parallel

    The same chapter explicitly says Herod's age limit for the killings was set according to the exact time learned from the magi. That later statement explains the narrative significance of this secret inquiry; it is an internal causal link.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 2:16 Then Herod, seeing that the magi had outwitted him, became exceedingly angry. He sent men and killed all the boys in Bethlehem and throughout its district who were two years old and under, according to the exact time he had learned from the magi.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:8

He sent them to Bethlehem, saying, “Go and inquire carefully about the Child. When you find Him, report to me, so that I too may come and bow before Him.”

  1. Matthew 2:16 narrative parallel

    Herod says he wishes to bow before the Child, but his subsequent order to kill the boys exposes the hostile purpose of his inquiry. This same-chapter comparison identifies the contrast between his stated pretext and his later action.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 2:16 Then Herod, seeing that the magi had outwitted him, became exceedingly angry. He sent men and killed all the boys in Bethlehem and throughout its district who were two years old and under, according to the exact time he had learned from the magi.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:9

After hearing the king, they went on their way. And behold, the star they had seen at its rising went ahead of them until it came and stood over the place where the Child was.

  1. Matthew 2:2 narrative parallel

    The magi identify the guiding star as the star they had already seen at its rising. The same-chapter reference to their earlier report establishes the recurrence of this particular sign; it supplies no independent account of the star's motion.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 2:2 saying, “Where is the One born King of the Jews? For we saw His star at its rising and have come to bow before Him.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:10

When they saw the star, they rejoiced with exceedingly great joy.

  1. Luke 2:10–2:11 thematic parallel

    The magi's exceedingly great joy at the sign of the Child corresponds to Luke's announcement of great joy because the Savior, Christ the Lord, has been born. The observers and signs differ, so this is a shared response to Jesus' birth rather than a merged visit.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 2:10 The angel said to them, “Do not fear, for behold, I announce to you good news of great joy, which will be for all the people:

    Luke 2:11 today in David’s city a Savior has been born for you, who is Christ the Lord.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:11

They entered the house and saw the Child with Mary His mother. Falling down, they bowed before Him; and opening their treasures, they offered Him gifts: gold, frankincense, and myrrh.

  1. Isaiah 60:5–60:6 allusion

    Isaiah pictures wealth coming from the nations and visitors carrying gold and frankincense. Matthew's visitors offer gold and frankincense to the Child, with myrrh added. The shared gift imagery supports an allusion, while Isaiah's address to Jerusalem is not erased.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 60:5 Then you will see and fear and be astonished in heart, for the wealth of sea, nations, and peoples will turn toward you. There will come to you

    Isaiah 60:6 herds of camels, and camels from Madiam and Gaipha will cover you. They will all come from Saba, bringing gold and carrying frankincense, and they will proclaim the Lord’s salvation as good news.

    Pinned target record

  2. Psalms 71:10–71:11 thematic parallel

    The Greek-numbered royal Psalm 71 pictures foreign kings bringing gifts and bowing before the king. Matthew's magi bow and offer treasures to the Child; Matthew calls them magi, and this comparison does not turn them into named or numbered kings.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 71:10 kings of Tarshish and the islands will offer gifts; kings of the Arabians and Saba will bring gifts.

    Psalms 71:11 And all kings will bow before him; all nations will serve him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:12

Having been warned in a dream not to return to Herod, they withdrew to their country by another way.

  1. Matthew 2:22 narrative parallel

    Matthew twice has a dream warning redirect a journey away from danger associated with Herod's dynasty: the magi avoid Herod here, and Joseph avoids Judea under Archelaus later. This is a same-book repeated protection motif involving different recipients.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 2:22 But hearing that Archelaus reigned over Judea in place of his father Herod, he feared to go there. Warned in a dream, he withdrew to the regions of Galilee.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:13

After they withdrew, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to Joseph in a dream, saying, “Rise, take the Child and His mother, and flee to Egypt. Stay there until I tell you, for Herod is about to seek the Child to destroy Him.”

  1. Exodus 1:16–1:22 narrative parallel

    Herod's threat to destroy the Child recalls Pharaoh's orders to kill Hebrew boys. This is a comparison of royal threats against male children, not a claim that the two rulers or historical events are identical.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 1:16 And he said, “When you attend the Hebrew women in childbirth, and they are about to give birth, if the child is male, kill him; but if the child is female, preserve her.”

    Exodus 1:17 But the midwives feared God. They did not do as the king of Egypt had commanded them, and they kept the boys alive.

    Exodus 1:18 And the king of Egypt called the midwives and said to them, “Why have you done this thing, and kept the male children alive?”

    Exodus 1:19 The midwives said to Pharaoh, “The Hebrew women are not like the Egyptian women, for they give birth before the midwives come to them.” And they bore children.

    Exodus 1:20 God dealt well with the midwives, and the people multiplied and became very strong.

    Exodus 1:21 And because the midwives feared God, they made houses for themselves.

    Exodus 1:22 And Pharaoh commanded all his people, saying, “Cast every male child born to the Hebrews into the river, but keep every female child alive.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:14

He rose, took the Child and His mother by night, and withdrew to Egypt.

  1. Genesis 46:2–46:4 narrative parallel

    Joseph takes his family into Egypt in response to the preceding dream instruction. Genesis similarly places Jacob's descent into Egypt under a divine night vision and a promise of return. The narratives concern different families and circumstances; their shared feature is a divinely directed family descent into Egypt.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 46:2 And God spoke to Israel in a vision of the night, saying, “Jacob, Jacob.” And he said, “What is it?”

    Genesis 46:3 And He said, “I am the God of your fathers. Do not fear to go down into Egypt, for I will make you a great nation there.

    Genesis 46:4 “And I will go down with you into Egypt, and I will bring you up at last; and Joseph shall lay his hands upon your eyes.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:15

He remained there until Herod’s death, so that what was spoken by the Lord through the prophet might be fulfilled: “Out of Egypt I called My Son.”

  1. Hosea 11:1 quotation

    Matthew applies the Egypt-call saying to Jesus' return in its fulfillment account. The pinned Saints Hosea text speaks of Israel as an infant and says 'out of Egypt I summoned his children,' whereas Matthew has 'I called My Son.' This is an identifiable quotation relationship with a real wording difference and Matthew's typological application, not an exact English match.

    Read matching passage

    Hosea 11:1 Because Israel was an infant, I loved him, and out of Egypt I summoned his children.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:16

Then Herod, seeing that the magi had outwitted him, became exceedingly angry. He sent men and killed all the boys in Bethlehem and throughout its district who were two years old and under, according to the exact time he had learned from the magi.

  1. Exodus 1:16–1:22 narrative parallel

    Both rulers command the killing of boys: Herod specifies Bethlehem and its district and the age of two and under, while Pharaoh's commands concern Hebrew newborn boys and, in the later order, drowning. The concrete parallel is targeted royal violence against male children; the details remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 1:16 And he said, “When you attend the Hebrew women in childbirth, and they are about to give birth, if the child is male, kill him; but if the child is female, preserve her.”

    Exodus 1:17 But the midwives feared God. They did not do as the king of Egypt had commanded them, and they kept the boys alive.

    Exodus 1:18 And the king of Egypt called the midwives and said to them, “Why have you done this thing, and kept the male children alive?”

    Exodus 1:19 The midwives said to Pharaoh, “The Hebrew women are not like the Egyptian women, for they give birth before the midwives come to them.” And they bore children.

    Exodus 1:20 God dealt well with the midwives, and the people multiplied and became very strong.

    Exodus 1:21 And because the midwives feared God, they made houses for themselves.

    Exodus 1:22 And Pharaoh commanded all his people, saying, “Cast every male child born to the Hebrews into the river, but keep every female child alive.”

Matthew 2:17

  1. Jeremiah 38:15 promise and fulfillment

    Matthew explicitly names Jeremiah when introducing the following Rachel lament. Its identifiable source is Jeremiah 38:15 in the Saints Greek-based numbering, and Matthew applies that lament to the aftermath of Herod's killings.

    Read matching passage

    Jeremiah 38:15 Thus said the Lord: A voice was heard in Ramah, lamentation, weeping, and wailing. Rachel, weeping aloud, would not stop over her sons, because they are no more.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 2:18

“A voice was heard in Ramah, weeping and great lamentation: Rachel weeping for her children, and she would not be comforted, because they are no more.”

  1. Jeremiah 38:15 quotation

    Both passages name Ramah and Rachel, describe weeping over her children or sons, and give the reason that they are no more. Matthew reuses Jeremiah's lament in the Bethlehem massacre narrative; the different English phrases about refusing comfort and not stopping are preserved.

    Read matching passage

    Jeremiah 38:15 Thus said the Lord: A voice was heard in Ramah, lamentation, weeping, and wailing. Rachel, weeping aloud, would not stop over her sons, because they are no more.

After Herod died, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared in a dream to Joseph in Egypt,

  1. Exodus 4:19 narrative parallel

saying, “Rise, take the Child and His mother, and go to the land of Israel, for those seeking the Child’s life have died.”

    The instruction to return because those seeking the Child's life have died closely echoes the Lord's direction to Moses to depart for Egypt because all who sought his life are dead. The destinations and persons differ, but the life-seekers-have-died wording is specific.

  1. Luke 2:39 narrative parallel

    Both accounts place the family in Galilee after the birth narrative, with Luke naming Nazareth as their own city. Luke does not report Archelaus or the dream warning, so the comparison supports the Galilean destination without claiming identical travel chronology.

  1. John 1:45–1:46 narrative parallel

    Matthew says the family settled in Nazareth and that Jesus would be called a Nazarene. John identifies Jesus as from Nazareth and records Nathanael's reaction to that origin. This supports Jesus' Nazareth identification; it does not identify a direct Old Testament quotation for Matthew's reference to the prophets.

    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

    Both birth narratives conclude with the family living in Nazareth in Galilee. Matthew presents the settlement after flight and return, whereas Luke summarizes the return after completion of the law's requirements; the cross-reference does not resolve the different narrative sequences.

    Jesus' answer cites Deuteronomy's contrast between living by bread alone and living by every word from God's mouth. Deuteronomy places the saying in Israel's experience of hunger and manna.

    Luke also records Jesus answering the bread temptation with the written saying that a human being does not live on bread alone. Luke's quoted wording in this edition ends there, without Matthew's additional clause about every word from God.

“Land of Zebulun and land of Naphtali, the way of the sea, beyond the Jordan, Galilee of the nations:

  1. Isaiah 8:23 quotation

    Matthew cites Isaiah's geographical sequence of Zebulun, Naphtali, the sea route, beyond the Jordan, and Galilee of the nations. The verse is Isaiah 8:23 in this edition; its longer wording continues beyond Matthew's excerpt.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 8:23 and the one in confinement will not remain perplexed for a time. “Do this first, do it quickly, country of Zebulun, land of Naphtali, way of the sea, and the rest who dwell by the sea and beyond the Jordan, Galilee of the nations, the regions of Judea.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:16

the people sitting in darkness saw a great light, and for those sitting in the region and shadow of death a light rose.”

  1. Isaiah 9:1 quotation

    Both texts announce great light for people in darkness and light for those in the land or shadow of death. Isaiah addresses people walking in darkness, whereas Matthew describes people sitting in darkness. This edition labels the Isaiah verse 9:1.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 9:1 You people who walk in darkness, behold a great light; you who dwell in a land and shadow of death, a light will shine upon you.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:17

From then on Jesus began to proclaim, “Repent, for the kingdom of the heavens has drawn near.”

  1. Mark 1:14–1:15 narrative parallel

    Both summarize the start of Jesus' Galilean proclamation with the nearness of God's kingdom and a call to repent. Mark also calls for belief in the gospel and says the appointed time is fulfilled.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:14 And after John was handed over, Jesus came into Galilee, proclaiming the gospel of God,

    Mark 1:15 and saying, “The appointed time is fulfilled, and the kingdom of God has drawn near; repent, and believe in the gospel.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:18

As He walked beside the Sea of Galilee, He saw two brothers, Simon called Peter and Andrew his brother, casting a net into the sea, for they were fishermen.

  1. Mark 1:16 narrative parallel

    Both accounts show Jesus beside the Sea of Galilee seeing the brothers Simon and Andrew casting a net, and both identify them as fishermen. Matthew additionally identifies Simon as Peter.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:16 And passing beside the Sea of Galilee, He saw Simon and Andrew the brother of Simon casting a net in the sea, for they were fishermen.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:19

He said to them, “Come after Me, and I will make you fishers of people.”

  1. Mark 1:17 narrative parallel

    Both record Jesus calling the fishermen to come after Him and promising to make them fishers of people or men.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:17 And He said to them, “Come after Me, and I will make you become fishers of men.”

    Pinned target record

  2. Luke 5:10 thematic parallel

    Jesus' promise of fishing for people corresponds to His promise to Simon in Luke that he will catch people alive. Luke places the saying after the extraordinary catch of fish, so the comparison does not assume identical narrative circumstances.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 5:10 and likewise James and John, Zebedee’s sons, who were Simon’s partners. Jesus said to Simon, “Do not fear. From now on you will be catching people alive.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:20

Immediately they left their nets and followed Him.

  1. Mark 1:18 narrative parallel

    Both describe the brothers' immediate response as leaving their nets and following Jesus.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:18 Immediately they left their nets and followed Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:21

Going on from there, He saw two other brothers, James the son of Zebedee and John his brother, in the boat with Zebedee their father, mending their nets; and He called them.

  1. Mark 1:19–1:20 narrative parallel

    Both identify James and John as Zebedee's sons, place them in a boat attending to their nets, and record Jesus calling them. Matthew places their father in the boat in this verse, while Mark mentions him in the response.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:19 Going a little farther, He saw James son of Zebedee and John his brother, who were also in the boat, putting the nets in order.

    Mark 1:20 Immediately He called them. They left their father Zebedee in the boat with the hired workers and went after Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:22

Immediately they left the boat and their father and followed Him.

  1. Mark 1:20 narrative parallel

    Both describe Zebedee's sons leaving their father in response to Jesus' call and going after Him. Mark additionally mentions hired workers in the boat.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:20 Immediately He called them. They left their father Zebedee in the boat with the hired workers and went after Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:23

He went throughout Galilee, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the good news of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every infirmity among the people.

  1. Matthew 9:35 thematic parallel

    The later Matthean summary repeats the combination of synagogue teaching, proclamation of the kingdom's good news, and healing every disease and infirmity; here the region is Galilee, while Matthew 9:35 refers to cities and villages.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 9:35 And Jesus went about all the cities and villages, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the gospel of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every infirmity.

    Pinned target record

  2. Mark 1:39 narrative parallel

    Both summarize Jesus traveling throughout Galilee and proclaiming in the synagogues. Matthew adds broad healing activity; Mark's summary explicitly mentions casting out demons.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:39 He went throughout all Galilee, proclaiming in their synagogues and casting out the demons.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:24

The report about Him spread throughout Syria, and they brought Him all who were ill, afflicted with various diseases and torments, those oppressed by demons, those suffering seizures, and paralytics; and He healed them.

  1. Luke 4:40–4:41 thematic parallel

    Both describe sick people being brought to Jesus, His healing varied diseases, and His dealing with demons. Luke gives an evening scene and the demons' cries; Matthew gives a wider regional summary including Syria, seizures, and paralysis, so this link compares the activity without equating the settings.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 4:40 As the sun was setting, everyone who had people sick with various diseases brought them to Him. He laid His hands on each one and healed them.

    Luke 4:41 Demons also came out of many, crying, “Thou art God’s Son!” He rebuked them and would not let them speak, because they knew He was the Christ.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 4:25

Great crowds followed Him from Galilee, the Decapolis, Jerusalem, Judea, and beyond the Jordan.

  1. Mark 3:7–3:8 thematic parallel

    Both crowd summaries name Galilee, Judea, Jerusalem, and beyond the Jordan as regions from which people followed or came to Jesus. Matthew additionally names the Decapolis; Mark additionally names Idumea and the Tyre-Sidon region. The overlapping geography does not prove a single occasion.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 3:7 Jesus withdrew with His disciples to the sea, and a great multitude from Galilee followed; and from Judea,

    Mark 3:8 Jerusalem, Idumea, beyond the Jordan, and the region of Tyre and Sidon, a great multitude heard all He was doing and came to Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:1

And seeing the crowds, He went up the mountain; and when He had sat down, His disciples came to Him.

  1. Luke 6:17–6:20 narrative parallel

    Both introduce Jesus teaching disciples in the presence of a crowd before the beatitudes. The settings differ: Matthew has Jesus ascend and sit on the mountain, whereas Luke has Him descend to a level place; the comparison does not erase that difference.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:17 He came down with them and stood on a level place. There was a large crowd of His disciples and a great multitude of the people from all Judea, Jerusalem, and the coastal region of Tyre and Sidon.

    Luke 6:18 They had come to hear Him and be healed of their diseases; those troubled by unclean spirits were also healed.

    Luke 6:19 The whole crowd sought to touch Him, because power went out from Him and healed everyone.

    Luke 6:20 Lifting His eyes toward His disciples, He said, “Blessed are you who are poor, because yours is God’s kingdom.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:2

And He opened His mouth and taught them, saying:

  1. Luke 6:20 teaching parallel

    Matthew introduces Jesus addressing the disciples and then beginning the beatitudes; Luke likewise directs His gaze and opening blessing toward His disciples.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:20 Lifting His eyes toward His disciples, He said, “Blessed are you who are poor, because yours is God’s kingdom.

Matthew 5:3

“Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of the heavens.

  1. Luke 6:20 teaching parallel

    Both pronounce the poor blessed and promise them the kingdom. Matthew specifies poor in spirit and kingdom of the heavens, while Luke addresses the poor and names God's kingdom.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:20 Lifting His eyes toward His disciples, He said, “Blessed are you who are poor, because yours is God’s kingdom.

Matthew 5:4

Blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted.

  1. Luke 6:21 teaching parallel

    Matthew promises comfort to mourners; Luke promises future laughter to those who weep now. Both blessings reverse present sorrow without using identical wording.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:21 Blessed are you who hunger now, because you will be filled. Blessed are you who weep now, because you will laugh.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:5

Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth.

  1. Psalms 36:11 allusion

    The promise that the meek will inherit the earth closely recalls the psalm's promise that the meek will inherit the land and delight in peace. The target is Greek-numbered Psalm 36 in this edition.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 36:11 But the meek will inherit the land and delight themselves in abundant peace.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:6

Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be filled.

  1. Luke 6:21 teaching parallel

    Both bless those who hunger and promise that they will be filled. Matthew explicitly makes righteousness the object of hunger and adds thirst; Luke speaks of those who hunger now.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:21 Blessed are you who hunger now, because you will be filled. Blessed are you who weep now, because you will laugh.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:7

Blessed are the merciful, for they shall receive mercy.

  1. James 2:13 thematic parallel

    Matthew connects showing mercy with receiving mercy; James warns that judgment is without mercy for the person who has shown none and says mercy triumphs over judgment.

    Read matching passage

    James 2:13 For judgment is without mercy to the one who has shown no mercy; mercy triumphs over judgment.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:8

Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God.

  1. Psalms 23:3–23:4 thematic parallel

    Matthew associates purity of heart with seeing God; the psalm describes the person with clean hands and a pure heart as qualified to stand in the Lord's holy place. This is a shared association between inward purity and access to God, not an identical promise.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 23:3 Who will ascend the Lord’s mountain, and who will stand in His holy place?

    Psalms 23:4 One innocent in hands and pure in heart, who did not take up his soul for emptiness and did not swear deceitfully to his neighbor.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:9

Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called sons of God.

  1. James 3:18 thematic parallel

    Matthew blesses peacemakers and calls them God's sons; James describes a harvest of righteousness sown in peace for those who make peace. The common subject is active peacemaking; James does not repeat the sonship promise.

    Read matching passage

    James 3:18 And a fruit of righteousness is sown in peace for those who make peace.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:10

Blessed are those who have been persecuted for righteousness’ sake, for theirs is the kingdom of the heavens.

  1. 1 Peter 3:14 teaching parallel

    Both passages pronounce people blessed when they suffer for righteousness. Matthew adds the kingdom promise, while Peter tells the sufferers not to fear.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 3:14 But even if you should suffer for righteousness, you are blessed. Do not fear their fear, and do not be troubled,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:11

Blessed are you when they revile you and persecute you and falsely speak every evil thing against you for My sake.

  1. Luke 6:22 teaching parallel

    Both bless disciples who are reviled and socially rejected because of their association with Jesus. Matthew emphasizes false evil accusations for His sake; Luke speaks of hatred and exclusion because of the Son of Man.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:22 Blessed are you when people hate you, when they exclude you, reproach you, and cast out your name as evil because of the Son of Man.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:12

Rejoice and be glad, for great is your reward in the heavens; for thus they persecuted the prophets before you.

  1. Luke 6:23 teaching parallel

    Both command joy amid persecution, promise a great heavenly reward, and compare the disciples' treatment with the persecution of earlier prophets.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:23 Rejoice on that day and leap, for behold, your reward is great in heaven; their fathers treated the prophets in the same way.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:13

You are the salt of the earth. But if the salt becomes tasteless, how shall it be made salty? It is no longer good for anything except to be thrown out and trampled underfoot by people.

  1. Luke 14:34–14:35 teaching parallel

    Both use salt that has lost its taste to describe something that can no longer serve its purpose and is thrown away. Matthew adds the disciples' identity as the earth's salt and the image of trampling.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 14:34 Salt, then, is good. But if even the salt becomes tasteless, with what will it be seasoned?

    Luke 14:35 It is fit neither for the soil nor for the manure heap; they throw it outside. Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:14

You are the light of the world. A city set upon a mountain cannot be hidden.

  1. Philippians 2:15 thematic parallel

    Matthew calls the disciples the world's light; Paul describes believers shining as lights in the world amid a crooked generation. Philippians shares the public witness image without repeating Matthew's hilltop city.

    Read matching passage

    Philippians 2:15 so that you may become blameless and innocent, children of God without blemish in the midst of a crooked and perverted generation, among whom you shine as lights in the world,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:15

Nor do people light a lamp and place it beneath the modius, but upon the lampstand; and it shines for everyone in the house.

  1. Luke 11:33 teaching parallel

    Both say a lit lamp belongs on a lampstand rather than under a modius so that people may see its light. The household lamp supplies the same illustration in each teaching.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 11:33 No one who lights a lamp puts it in a hidden place or under the modius basket, but on the lampstand, so that those who enter may see its light.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:16

So let your light shine before people, that they may see your good works and glorify your Father who is in the heavens.

  1. 1 Peter 2:12 teaching parallel

    Both connect visible good works with other people glorifying God. Matthew tells disciples to let their light shine, while Peter applies good conduct to a setting where outsiders slander believers.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 2:12 keeping your conduct good among the Gentiles, so that, in the very matter for which they slander you as evildoers, they may observe your good works and glorify God on the day of visitation.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:17

Do not think that I came to abolish the Law or the Prophets. I did not come to abolish, but to fulfil.

  1. Romans 3:31 thematic parallel

    Jesus denies abolishing the Law and Prophets and speaks of fulfillment; Paul likewise denies that faith nullifies law and says it establishes law. The comparison concerns refusal to discard the law, without treating the two arguments as identical.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 3:31 Do we then nullify law through faith? May it never be! Rather, we establish law.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:18

For truly I tell you: until heaven and earth pass away, not one iota nor one stroke shall pass from the Law, until all things come to pass.

  1. Luke 16:17 teaching parallel

    Both contrast the passing of heaven and earth with the enduring smallest written detail of the Law. Luke's small stroke corresponds to Matthew's iota and stroke.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 16:17 But it is easier for heaven and earth to pass away than for one small stroke of the Law to fall.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:19

Whoever therefore loosens one of these least commandments and teaches people to do so shall be called least in the kingdom of the heavens; but whoever does and teaches them shall be called great in the kingdom of the heavens.

  1. James 2:10 thematic parallel

    Matthew warns against loosening even a least commandment; James says stumbling in one point makes a person accountable for the whole law. Both resist treating part of God's law as dispensable.

    Read matching passage

    James 2:10 For whoever keeps the whole law but stumbles in one point has become accountable for all of it.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:20

For I tell you: unless your righteousness exceeds that of the scribes and Pharisees, you shall by no means enter the kingdom of the heavens.

  1. Matthew 23:25–23:28 thematic parallel

    The later denunciation explains a concrete defect in the scribes' and Pharisees' righteousness: an outwardly righteous appearance can conceal hypocrisy and lawlessness. It helps illuminate the demand for righteousness exceeding theirs without equating every member of those groups with the criticism.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 23:25 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you cleanse the outside of the cup and dish, but inside they are full of plunder and self-indulgence!

    Matthew 23:26 Blind Pharisee, first cleanse the inside of the cup, so that its outside may also become clean.

    Matthew 23:27 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you resemble whitewashed tombs, which outwardly appear beautiful, but inwardly are full of dead people’s bones and all uncleanness!

    Matthew 23:28 So you too outwardly appear righteous to people, but inwardly you are full of hypocrisy and lawlessness.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:21

You have heard that it was said to those of old: ‘You shall not murder’; and ‘whoever murders shall be liable to judgment.’

  1. Exodus 20:15 quotation

    The command not to murder repeats the Decalogue prohibition. In this Saints Greek-based inventory it is Exodus 20:15; the command itself does not include Matthew's following judicial explanation.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 20:15 You shall not murder.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:22

But I tell you that everyone who is angry with his brother shall be liable to judgment; and whoever says to his brother, ‘Raca,’ shall be liable to the council; and whoever says, ‘Fool,’ shall be liable to the Gehenna of fire.

  1. 1 John 3:15 teaching parallel

    Matthew brings anger toward a brother under judgment; John calls hatred of a brother murder. Both extend concern beyond the outward act of homicide to the inward hostility behind it.

    Read matching passage

    1 John 3:15 Everyone who hates his brother is a murderer, and you know that no murderer has eternal life abiding in him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:23

If therefore you offer your gift upon the altar, and there remember that your brother has something against you,

  1. Mark 11:25 thematic parallel

    Both bring damaged relationships into the setting of worship. Matthew asks the offerer to remember a brother's grievance, while Mark tells a praying person to forgive his own grievance against another; the directions of the grievance differ.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 11:25 Whenever you stand praying, forgive if you have anything against anyone, so that your Father in the heavens may also forgive your trespasses.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:24

leave your gift there before the altar, and go; first be reconciled to your brother, and then come and offer your gift.

  1. Romans 12:18 thematic parallel

    Matthew requires the worshiper first to seek reconciliation with his brother; Paul tells believers, as far as it depends on them, to live at peace with everyone. Both demand active efforts toward peaceful relationships.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 12:18 If possible, as far as it depends on you, live at peace with all people.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:25

Come to terms with your adversary quickly, while you are with him on the way, lest your adversary hand you over to the judge, and the judge to the officer, and you be thrown into prison.

  1. Luke 12:58 teaching parallel

    Both urge reaching a settlement with an adversary while still on the way, before the adversary hands the debtor to a judge and officer and imprisonment follows.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 12:58 For as you go with your adversary before a ruler, make an effort on the way to be released from him, lest he drag you before the judge, and the judge hand you over to the officer, and the officer throw you into prison.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:26

Truly I tell you: you shall by no means come out of there until you have paid the last quadrans.

  1. Luke 12:59 teaching parallel

    Both conclude the adversary-and-prison saying by warning that release will wait until the smallest outstanding coin is paid. Matthew names a quadrans and Luke a lepton.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 12:59 I tell you, you will certainly not come out from there until you have paid even the last lepton.”

Matthew 5:27

You have heard that it was said: ‘You shall not commit adultery.’

  1. Exodus 20:13 quotation

    The prohibition of adultery repeats the Decalogue command. This Saints Greek-based edition places it at Exodus 20:13, rather than silently adopting another ordering of the commandments.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 20:13 You shall not commit adultery.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:28

But I tell you that everyone who looks at a woman to lust after her has already committed adultery with her in his heart.

  1. Job 31:1 thematic parallel

    Matthew condemns a lustful look as inward adultery; Job describes a covenant governing his eyes so that he will not direct his attention toward a virgin. Both connect sexual integrity with disciplined looking, though Job does not formulate Matthew's judgment of the heart.

    Read matching passage

    Job 31:1 “I established a covenant for my eyes, and I will not direct my attention toward a virgin.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:29

If your right eye causes you to stumble, pluck it out and throw it away from you; for it is better for you that one of your members perish than that your whole body be thrown into Gehenna.

  1. Mark 9:47–9:48 teaching parallel

    Both use the severe image of discarding an eye that causes stumbling and contrast losing one member with being thrown into Gehenna. Mark continues with the description of undying worm and fire.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 9:47 If your eye causes you to stumble, cast it out. It is better for you to enter the kingdom of God with one eye than to have two eyes and be thrown into Gehenna,

    Mark 9:48 where their worm does not die and the fire is not extinguished.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:30

And if your right hand causes you to stumble, cut it off and throw it away from you; for it is better for you that one of your members perish than that your whole body go into Gehenna.

  1. Mark 9:43 teaching parallel

    Both use cutting off a hand that causes stumbling to stress the greater danger of Gehenna. Matthew contrasts the loss of one member with the whole body; Mark contrasts entering life maimed with retaining two hands.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 9:43 If your hand causes you to stumble, cut it off. It is better for you to enter life maimed than to have two hands and go away into Gehenna, into the unquenchable fire.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:31

And it was said: ‘Whoever divorces his wife, let him give her a certificate of divorce.’

  1. Deuteronomy 24:1 quotation

    Matthew's saying about giving a certificate of divorce recalls the certificate in Deuteronomy's case law. Deuteronomy describes the written certificate within a legal case; this comparison does not turn that description into an unrestricted command to divorce.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 24:1 And if a man takes a wife and dwells with her as husband, and she finds no favour in his sight, because he has found some unseemly thing in her, and he writes her a certificate of divorce, and gives it into her hands, and sends her forth out of his house,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:32

But I tell you that everyone who divorces his wife, except on account of sexual immorality, causes her to commit adultery; and whoever marries a divorced woman commits adultery.

  1. Luke 16:18 teaching parallel

    Both connect divorce and remarriage with adultery and specifically mention marrying a divorced woman. Matthew includes a sexual-immorality qualification and the husband's responsibility for causing adultery; Luke's wording here contains neither qualification.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 16:18 Everyone who divorces his wife and marries another commits adultery, and the one who marries a woman divorced from a husband commits adultery.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:33

Again, you have heard that it was said to those of old: ‘You shall not swear falsely, but shall fulfil your oaths to the Lord.’

  1. Leviticus 19:12 allusion

    Matthew's prohibition of swearing falsely recalls Leviticus's ban on swearing by God's name for an unjust cause and profaning His name. The shared concern is misuse of an oath invoking God.

    Read matching passage

    Leviticus 19:12 And you shall not swear by My name for an unjust cause, and you shall not profane the name of your God. I am the Lord your God.

    Pinned target record

  2. Deuteronomy 23:22–23:24 allusion

    Matthew's instruction to fulfill oaths to the Lord corresponds to Deuteronomy's requirement not to delay paying a vow and to do what has proceeded from one's lips. Deuteronomy also distinguishes a voluntarily unmade vow from a vow that binds its maker.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 23:22 And if you vow a vow unto the Lord your God, you shall not delay to pay it; for the Lord your God shall surely require it of you, and there shall be sin upon you.

    Deuteronomy 23:23 But if you do not desire to vow, there is no sin upon you.

    Deuteronomy 23:24 That which proceeds through your lips you shall keep and do, as you have vowed a gift unto the Lord your God, which you have spoken with your mouth.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:34

But I tell you: do not swear at all, neither by heaven, for it is the throne of God,

  1. Isaiah 66:1 allusion

    Jesus explains that heaven must not be used as an oath object because it is God's throne; Isaiah explicitly calls heaven the Lord's throne. The throne image supplies the stated reason, while Isaiah is not itself an oath prohibition.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 66:1 Thus says the Lord: “Heaven is My throne, and earth the footstool of My feet. What sort of house will you build for Me, or what place is My rest?

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:35

nor by the earth, for it is the footstool of His feet, nor by Jerusalem, for it is the city of the great King.

  1. Isaiah 66:1 allusion

    Matthew's description of earth as the footstool of God's feet closely recalls Isaiah's description of earth as His footstool. Jesus applies the image to the prohibition on swearing by the earth.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 66:1 Thus says the Lord: “Heaven is My throne, and earth the footstool of My feet. What sort of house will you build for Me, or what place is My rest?

    Pinned target record

  2. Psalms 47:3 allusion

    The designation of Jerusalem as the city of the great King recalls the psalm's description of Zion as the city of the great King. The target retains this edition's Greek numbering, Psalm 47:3.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 47:3 taking good root for the exultation of all the earth. Zion’s mountains, the sides of the north, the city of the great King.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:36

Nor shall you swear by your head, for you cannot make one hair white or black.

  1. James 5:12 teaching parallel

    Matthew forbids swearing by one's head and gives human inability to control even one hair's color as a reason; James forbids any other oath in addition to heaven and earth. The general oath prohibition corresponds, though James does not repeat the hair illustration.

    Read matching passage

    James 5:12 But above all, my brothers, do not swear, either by heaven or by earth or by any other oath. Let your yes be yes, and your no be no, so that you may not fall under judgment.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:37

But let your word be ‘Yes, yes’; ‘No, no.’ Whatever is beyond these is of evil.

  1. James 5:12 teaching parallel

    Both replace oath-taking with a dependable yes and no. James repeats the paired yes and no and connects the instruction with avoiding judgment.

    Read matching passage

    James 5:12 But above all, my brothers, do not swear, either by heaven or by earth or by any other oath. Let your yes be yes, and your no be no, so that you may not fall under judgment.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:38

You have heard that it was said: ‘An eye for an eye, and a tooth for a tooth.’

  1. Exodus 21:24 quotation

    Matthew's eye-for-eye and tooth-for-tooth formula repeats the matching clauses in Exodus's legal rule of corresponding injury. The target also continues the series with hand and foot.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 21:24 eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand, foot for foot,

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:39

But I tell you: do not resist the evildoer; but whoever strikes you upon your right cheek, turn the other to him also.

  1. Luke 6:29 teaching parallel

    Both instruct a person struck on one cheek to offer the other. Matthew places the instruction under not resisting the evildoer and specifies the right cheek.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:29 Offer the other cheek also to the one striking you on the cheek; and from the one taking your cloak, do not withhold your tunic also.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:40

And if anyone would sue you and take your tunic, let him have your cloak also.

  1. Luke 6:29 teaching parallel

    Both pair tunic and cloak in an instruction to surrender more than an opponent takes. Matthew begins with a lawsuit over the tunic and adds the cloak; Luke begins with the cloak and says not to withhold the tunic.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:29 Offer the other cheek also to the one striking you on the cheek; and from the one taking your cloak, do not withhold your tunic also.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:41

And whoever compels you to go one Roman mile, go with him two.

  1. Matthew 27:32 thematic parallel: compulsory service

    Both passages explicitly describe being compelled to perform service. The instruction about going a Roman mile is compared with Simon being compelled to carry the cross. The cross-bearing account supplies neither a mile distance nor a second-mile response; this is a concrete thematic comparison, not an identification of commands or occasions.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 27:32 As they went out, they found a Cyrenian man named Simon. They compelled him to carry His cross.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:42

Give to the one who asks you, and do not turn away from the one who would borrow from you.

  1. Luke 6:30 teaching parallel

    Both tell disciples to give to the person who asks. Matthew adds not turning away a prospective borrower; Luke adds not demanding possessions back from someone who takes them.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:30 Give to everyone who asks you, and do not demand back your belongings from the one who takes them.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:43

You have heard that it was said: ‘You shall love your neighbour and hate your enemy.’

  1. Leviticus 19:18 quotation

    The love-your-neighbor portion recalls Leviticus's command to love the neighbor as oneself. Leviticus also forbids vengeance and lingering anger; it does not supply Matthew's accompanying hate-your-enemy clause.

    Read matching passage

    Leviticus 19:18 And your hand shall not take vengeance, neither shall you remain angry with the sons of your people; and you shall love your neighbour as yourself; I am the Lord.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:44

But I tell you: love your enemies, and pray for those who persecute you,

  1. Luke 6:27–6:28 teaching parallel

    Both command love for enemies and prayer for those who mistreat the disciples. Luke also specifies doing good to haters and blessing those who curse.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:27 But I say to you who hear: Love your enemies; do good to those who hate you.

    Luke 6:28 Bless those who curse you; pray for those who mistreat you.

Matthew 5:45

that you may become sons of your Father who is in the heavens; for He makes His sun rise upon the evil and the good, and sends rain upon the righteous and the unrighteous.

  1. Luke 6:35 teaching parallel

    Both connect loving enemies with becoming children of God and ground that conduct in God's kindness toward evil people. Matthew illustrates indiscriminate beneficence with sunshine and rain; Luke mentions kindness to the ungrateful and evil.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:35 But love your enemies, do good, and lend, expecting nothing back. Your reward will be great, and you will be sons of the Most High, because He is kind to the ungrateful and evil.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:46

For if you love those who love you, what reward do you have? Do not even the tax collectors do the same?

  1. Luke 6:32 teaching parallel

    Both ask what reward or credit there is in loving only those who return love, since people outside the disciples' community also do that. Matthew uses tax collectors as the comparison; Luke uses sinners.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:32 If you love those who love you, what credit is that to you? For even sinners love those who love them.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 5:47

And if you greet only your brothers, what are you doing beyond others? Do not even the Gentiles do the same?

  1. Luke 6:33 teaching parallel

    Both challenge conduct limited to reciprocating ordinary social goodwill. Matthew's example is greeting only brothers, while Luke's is doing good only to those who do good in return; these are corresponding arguments with different examples.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:33 If you do good to those who do good to you, what credit is that to you? Even sinners do the same.

  1. Luke 6:36 teaching parallel

    Both end the enemy-love teaching by calling disciples to model their conduct on their Father. Matthew uses perfect and Luke uses merciful; the wording and emphasis remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:36 Be merciful, just as your Father is merciful.

  1. Proverbs 19:17 thematic parallel

    Matthew promises the Father's repayment for alms given in secret; Proverbs says the giver who has mercy on the poor lends to God and will be repaid by Him. The shared point is divine repayment, while secrecy is specific to Matthew.

Matthew 6:5

  1. Matthew 23:5–23:6 thematic parallel

    Matthew 6 criticizes prayers staged for public visibility in synagogues and streets; the later passage criticizes works performed to be seen and love of prominent synagogue seats. Both expose religious conduct arranged for public honor.

Matthew 6:6

But you, when you pray, enter your inner room, shut your door, and pray to your Father who is in secret; and your Father who sees in secret will repay you.

  1. 2 Kings 4:33 thematic parallel

    Jesus instructs a person to shut the door and pray privately; Elisha is narrated entering the house, shutting the door behind himself and the child, and praying to the Lord. The shared action illustrates secluded prayer without making Elisha's emergency identical to the command's setting.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 4:33 Elisha entered the house, shut the door behind the two of them, and prayed to the Lord.

Matthew 6:7

When praying, do not babble like the Gentiles, for they think they will be heard through their many words.

  1. Ecclesiastes 5:1–5:2 thematic parallel

    Matthew rejects the idea that many words secure a hearing from God; Ecclesiastes urges few words before God and associates a fool's voice with many words. Both caution against unrestrained religious speech.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 5:1 Do not be hasty with your mouth, and do not let your heart hurry to bring out a word before God, for God is in heaven and you are on earth. Therefore let your words be few.

    Ecclesiastes 5:2 For a dream comes with much distraction, and a fool’s voice with many words.

  1. Mark 11:25 teaching parallel

    Matthew states negatively the condition that Mark expresses positively: refusing to forgive others is incompatible with receiving the Father's forgiveness of one's own trespasses. Mark does not separately repeat Matthew's negative sentence.

  1. Isaiah 58:5 thematic parallel

    Matthew rejects conspicuously miserable fasting staged for human recognition; Isaiah rejects the assumption that bowed posture, sackcloth, and ashes by themselves constitute an acceptable fast. Both challenge outward displays of fasting, though Isaiah's passage focuses on the fast God chooses.

Matthew 6:34

So do not be anxious about tomorrow, for tomorrow will be anxious about itself. Sufficient for the day is its own trouble.

  1. Proverbs 27:1 thematic parallel

    Both warn about one's attitude toward tomorrow. Matthew rejects anxiety and says the present day has sufficient trouble; Proverbs rejects boasting about tomorrow because the coming day's events are unknown. This is a limited thematic comparison between different responses to the future, not an equation of anxiety with boasting.
