Not everyone who says to Me, ‘Lord, Lord,’ will enter the kingdom of the heavens, but the one who does the will of My Father in the heavens.

  1. Luke 6:46 teaching parallel

    Both deny that addressing Jesus as Lord is sufficient when obedience is absent. Matthew requires doing the Father's will; Luke asks why people say Lord, Lord while failing to do what He says.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:46 Why do you call Me ‘Lord, Lord,’ and not do what I say?

    Pinned target record

Matthew 7:22

Many will say to Me on that day, ‘Lord, Lord, did we not prophesy in Thy name, cast out demons in Thy name, and do many mighty works in Thy name?’

  1. 1 Corinthians 13:2 thematic parallel

    Matthew depicts people appealing to prophecy and mighty works as evidence of standing with Jesus; Paul says that even prophecy and mountain-moving faith amount to nothing without love. Both challenge reliance on extraordinary religious gifts alone, though Paul does not repeat the judgment scene or the claim about exorcisms.

    Read matching passage

    1 Corinthians 13:2 And if I have prophecy and know all mysteries and all knowledge, and if I have all faith so as to move mountains, but have no love, I am nothing.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 7:23

Then I will declare to them, ‘I never knew you. Depart from Me, you who practice lawlessness.’

  1. Luke 13:27 teaching parallel

    Both portray a decisive refusal of people who claim association with the Lord, followed by a command that workers of wrongdoing depart. Matthew says I never knew you; Luke says I do not know you or where you come from.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 13:27 And he will say to you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from. Depart from me, all you workers of unrighteousness!’

    Pinned target record

  2. Psalms 6:9 allusion

    The command for workers of lawlessness to depart recalls the psalmist's command that all workers of lawlessness stand away from him. Matthew places the declaration on Jesus' lips in the judgment scene; this edition numbers the target Psalm 6:9.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 6:9 Stand away from me, all who work lawlessness, for the Lord heard the voice of my weeping.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 7:24

Therefore everyone who hears these words of Mine and puts them into practice will be likened to a prudent man who built his house upon the rock.

  1. Luke 6:47–6:48 teaching parallel

    Both compare a person who hears Jesus' words and acts on them to a builder who establishes a house on rock. Luke adds the detail of digging deeply for the foundation.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:47 Everyone who comes to Me, hears My words and does them—I will show you whom he resembles.

    Luke 6:48 He resembles a man building a house, who dug and went deep and laid a foundation on the rock. When a flood came, the river burst against that house but could not shake it, because it had been well built.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 7:25

The rain came down, the rivers came, and the winds blew and fell upon that house, and it did not fall, for it had been founded upon the rock.

  1. Luke 6:48 teaching parallel

    Both describe a house remaining secure when a flood or river strikes because it has been properly founded. Matthew includes rain and wind and explicitly repeats that the foundation was rock.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:48 He resembles a man building a house, who dug and went deep and laid a foundation on the rock. When a flood came, the river burst against that house but could not shake it, because it had been well built.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 7:26

And everyone who hears these words of Mine and does not put them into practice will be likened to a foolish man who built his house upon the sand.

  1. Luke 6:49 teaching parallel

    Both compare hearing without acting to an unsound house. Matthew has a foolish builder on sand; Luke describes a house built on the ground without a foundation.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:49 But the one who heard and did not act resembles a man who built a house on the ground without a foundation. The river burst against it, and immediately it collapsed; the ruin of that house was great.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 7:27

The rain came down, the rivers came, and the winds blew and struck that house, and it fell; and its fall was great.”

  1. Luke 6:49 teaching parallel

    Both conclude that the house corresponding to hearing without obedience collapses when the river strikes, and both emphasize how great its ruin is. Matthew also lists rain and wind.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:49 But the one who heard and did not act resembles a man who built a house on the ground without a foundation. The river burst against it, and immediately it collapsed; the ruin of that house was great.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 7:28

When Jesus finished these words, the crowds were astonished at His teaching,

  1. Mark 1:22 narrative parallel

    Both narrate the audience's astonishment at Jesus' teaching. Matthew reports it after the completed sermon; Mark reports it in the Capernaum synagogue setting.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:22 They were astonished at His teaching, for He taught them as one having authority, and not as the scribes.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 7:29

for He taught them as One having authority, and not as their scribes.

  1. Mark 1:22 narrative parallel

    Both explain astonishment by contrasting Jesus' teaching with that of the scribes: He teaches as one having authority. The common evaluation is retained while the narrative occasions differ.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:22 They were astonished at His teaching, for He taught them as one having authority, and not as the scribes.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:2

And behold, a leper came and bowed before Him, saying, “Lord, if Thou art willing, Thou canst cleanse me.”

  1. Mark 1:40 narrative parallel

    Both narrate a leper approaching Jesus and saying that Jesus can cleanse him if willing. Matthew describes bowing before Him, while Mark describes pleading and kneeling.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:40 A leper came to Him, pleading with Him, kneeling, and saying, “If Thou art willing, Thou canst cleanse me.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:3

He stretched out His hand and touched him, saying, “I am willing; be cleansed.” Immediately his leprosy was cleansed.

  1. Mark 1:41–1:42 narrative parallel

    Both describe Jesus touching the leper, declaring His willingness and commanding cleansing, followed immediately by the disappearance of leprosy.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:41 And moved with compassion, He stretched out His hand and touched him, and says to him, “I will; be cleansed.”

    Mark 1:42 Immediately the leprosy departed from him, and he was cleansed.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:4

Jesus said to him, “See that you tell no one, but go, show yourself to the priest, and offer the gift Moses commanded as a testimony to them.”

  1. Mark 1:43–1:44 narrative parallel

    Both record a strict instruction not to publicize the healing, followed by showing oneself to the priest and making Moses' prescribed offering as testimony.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:43 Sternly warning him, He immediately drove him away

    Mark 1:44 and said to him, “See that you say nothing to anyone. But go, show yourself to the priest, and offer for your cleansing what Moses commanded, as a testimony to them.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:5

When He entered Capernaum, a centurion came to Him, pleading with Him

  1. Luke 7:1–7:3 narrative parallel

    Both place a centurion's appeal for his servant in Capernaum. Matthew presents the centurion approaching Jesus directly; Luke narrates the appeal through Jewish elders sent by him. The modes of approach are not silently harmonized.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:1 When He had finished all His sayings in the people’s hearing, He entered Capernaum.

    Luke 7:2 A certain centurion had a slave who was ill and about to die, and who was precious to him.

    Luke 7:3 Hearing about Jesus, he sent elders of the Jews to Him, asking Him to come and save his slave.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:6

and saying, “Lord, my servant lies at home paralyzed, terribly tormented.”

  1. Luke 7:2 narrative parallel

    Both identify a centurion's seriously ill servant as the reason for the appeal. Matthew specifies paralysis and severe suffering, whereas Luke says the valued slave was ill and about to die.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:2 A certain centurion had a slave who was ill and about to die, and who was precious to him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:7

He said to him, “I will come and heal him.”

  1. Luke 7:6 narrative parallel

    Jesus' stated readiness to come in Matthew corresponds to His accompanying the elders toward the centurion's house in Luke. Luke's communication proceeds through messengers rather than Matthew's direct dialogue.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:6 Jesus went with them. When He was already not far from the house, the centurion sent friends, saying to Him, “Lord, do not trouble Thyself, for I am not worthy for Thee to enter under my roof.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:8

The centurion answered, “Lord, I am not fit for Thee to enter under my roof. Only speak a word, and my servant will be healed.

  1. Luke 7:6–7:7 narrative parallel

    Both give the centurion's declaration that he is unworthy to have Jesus enter his roof and that a word will suffice to heal the servant. Luke delivers the declaration through friends and adds his unworthiness to approach personally.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:6 Jesus went with them. When He was already not far from the house, the centurion sent friends, saying to Him, “Lord, do not trouble Thyself, for I am not worthy for Thee to enter under my roof.

    Luke 7:7 Therefore I did not even consider myself worthy to come to Thee. But speak a word, and let my servant be healed.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:9

For I too am a man under authority, with soldiers under me. I say to this one, ‘Go,’ and he goes; to another, ‘Come,’ and he comes; and to my slave, ‘Do this,’ and he does it.”

  1. Luke 7:8 narrative parallel

    Both use the centurion's position under authority and his commands to soldiers and a slave as the concrete analogy for the efficacy of Jesus' command.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:8 For I too am a man placed under authority, with soldiers under me. I tell this one, ‘Go,’ and he goes; another, ‘Come,’ and he comes; and my slave, ‘Do this,’ and he does it.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:10

When Jesus heard this, He marveled and said to those following, “Truly I tell you, with no one in Israel have I found such faith.

  1. Luke 7:9 narrative parallel

    Both narrate Jesus marveling at the centurion's faith and declaring to the following crowd that He has not found such faith even in Israel.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:9 When Jesus heard this, He marveled at him. Turning to the crowd following Him, He said, “I tell you, not even in Israel have I found such faith.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:11

I tell you that many will come from east and west and recline with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the kingdom of the heavens,

  1. Luke 13:28–13:29 teaching parallel

    Both picture people arriving from distant directions to recline in God's kingdom alongside the patriarchs Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. Luke also names north and south and includes the prophets.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 13:28 There will be weeping and grinding of teeth there, when you see Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of God, but yourselves being thrown outside.

    Luke 13:29 And people will come from east and west and from north and south and recline at table in the kingdom of God.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:12

but the sons of the kingdom will be cast into the outer darkness. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.”

  1. Luke 13:28 teaching parallel

    Both contrast inclusion with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob in the kingdom with exclusion accompanied by weeping and grinding of teeth. Matthew calls the excluded sons of the kingdom and adds outer darkness; Luke addresses those cast outside.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 13:28 There will be weeping and grinding of teeth there, when you see Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of God, but yourselves being thrown outside.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:13

Jesus said to the centurion, “Go; as you have believed, let it happen for you.” And his servant was healed at that hour.

  1. Luke 7:10 narrative parallel

    Both conclude the centurion episode with the servant well. Matthew directly joins healing to Jesus' response to the centurion's faith and specifies that hour; Luke reports the messengers finding the slave healthy on returning home.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:10 Those who had been sent returned to the house and found the slave in good health.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:14

Jesus entered Peter’s house and saw his mother-in-law lying down with a fever.

  1. Mark 1:29–1:30 narrative parallel

    Both place the healing in Simon Peter's household and identify his mother-in-law as lying ill with a fever. Mark also names Andrew's house and the accompanying James and John.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:29 Immediately after leaving the synagogue, they went into the house of Simon and Andrew, with James and John.

    Mark 1:30 Simon’s mother-in-law was lying down with a fever, and immediately they told Him about her.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:15

He touched her hand, and the fever left her. She arose and served Him.

  1. Mark 1:31 narrative parallel

    Both describe Jesus taking or touching the woman's hand, the fever leaving, and her rising to serve. Matthew says she served Him, while Mark says she served them.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:31 He came to her, took her by the hand, and raised her up. The fever left her, and she served them.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:16

When evening came, they brought Him many who were oppressed by demons. He cast out the spirits with a word and healed all who were ill,

  1. Mark 1:32–1:34 narrative parallel

    Both describe people bringing sick and demon-afflicted persons at evening, followed by healings and expulsions of demons. Matthew says He healed all who were ill; Mark's summary says He healed many and adds the prohibition on demons speaking.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:32 When evening came, after the sun had set, they brought to Him all who were ill and those possessed by demons.

    Mark 1:33 The whole city had gathered at the door.

    Mark 1:34 He healed many who were ill with various diseases and cast out many demons. He would not allow the demons to speak, because they knew Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:17

so that what was spoken through Isaiah the prophet might be fulfilled: “He took our weaknesses and carried our diseases.”

  1. Isaiah 53:4 quotation

    Matthew explicitly applies Isaiah's servant passage to Jesus' healings. Its wording about taking weaknesses and carrying diseases differs from this Saints Isaiah text, which says the servant bears our sins and suffers pain for us; the connection is identified without replacing either English wording.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 53:4 He bears our sins, and suffers pain for us; yet we reckoned him to be in toil and affliction and distress.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:18

Seeing a crowd around Him, Jesus gave orders to depart to the other side.

  1. Mark 4:35–4:36 narrative parallel

    Both introduce the lake crossing by Jesus directing departure to the other side and leaving the crowd. Mark places the direction in the evening and describes taking Him in the boat.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 4:35 On that day, when evening came, He said to them, “Let us cross to the other side.”

    Mark 4:36 Leaving the crowd, they took Him with them, just as He was, in the boat. Other boats were with Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:19

One scribe came and said to Him, “Teacher, I will follow Thee wherever Thou goest.”

  1. Luke 9:57 teaching parallel

    Both record a prospective follower promising to follow Jesus wherever He goes. Matthew identifies the speaker as a scribe, whereas Luke leaves him unnamed and places the exchange on the road.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 9:57 As they traveled on the road, someone said to Him, “I will follow Thee wherever Thou goest.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:20

Jesus said to him, “Foxes have dens and the birds of the sky have resting places, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay His head.”

  1. Luke 9:58 teaching parallel

    Both contrast the shelters of foxes and birds with the Son of Man's having nowhere to lay His head in the response to the prospective follower.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 9:58 Jesus said to him, “Foxes have dens and the birds of the sky have dwelling places, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay His head.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:21

Another of His disciples said to Him, “Lord, first permit me to go and bury my father.”

  1. Luke 9:59 teaching parallel

    Both present a would-be follower asking first to leave and bury his father. Luke explicitly begins with Jesus' call to follow, while Matthew identifies the speaker as another disciple.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 9:59 He said to another, “Follow Me.” But he said, “Lord, permit me first to go and bury my father.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:22

But Jesus said to him, “Follow Me, and let the dead bury their own dead.”

  1. Luke 9:60 teaching parallel

    Both give the striking instruction to let the dead bury their own dead. Matthew joins it to following Jesus; Luke adds the commission to proclaim God's kingdom.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 9:60 He said to him, “Let the dead bury their own dead; but you go and proclaim God’s kingdom.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:23

And when He entered the boat, His disciples followed Him.

  1. Mark 4:36 narrative parallel

    Both put Jesus and His followers in a boat at the start of the storm crossing. Mark describes leaving the crowd and other boats accompanying Him, details absent from Matthew's short boarding notice.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 4:36 Leaving the crowd, they took Him with them, just as He was, in the boat. Other boats were with Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:24

And behold, a great tempest arose in the sea, so that the boat was covered by the waves; but He was sleeping.

  1. Mark 4:37–4:38 narrative parallel

    Both place Jesus asleep in a boat endangered by a severe storm and waves. Mark specifies His place in the stern on a cushion and says water was already filling the boat.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 4:37 A great windstorm arose, and the waves broke into the boat, so that the boat was already filling.

    Mark 4:38 He was in the stern, asleep on the cushion. They woke Him and said to Him, “Teacher, dost Thou not care that we are perishing?”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:25

And they came to Him and awoke Him, saying, “Lord, save; we are perishing.”

  1. Mark 4:38 narrative parallel

    Both have the disciples wake Jesus because they are perishing. Matthew addresses Him as Lord and asks salvation; Mark addresses Him as Teacher and asks whether He cares.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 4:38 He was in the stern, asleep on the cushion. They woke Him and said to Him, “Teacher, dost Thou not care that we are perishing?”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:26

And He says to them, “Why are you fearful, you of little faith?” Then He arose and rebuked the winds and the sea; and there was a great calm.

  1. Mark 4:39–4:40 narrative parallel

    Both connect Jesus' rebuke of wind and sea with great calm and a rebuke concerning the disciples' fear and faith. Matthew places the question before the calming; Mark places it afterward.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 4:39 And He awoke, rebuked the wind, and said to the sea, “Be silent; be still.” And the wind ceased, and there was a great calm.

    Mark 4:40 He said to them, “Why are you cowardly? Do you still have no faith?”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:27

And the men marvelled, saying, “What kind of man is this, that even the winds and the sea obey Him?”

  1. Mark 4:41 narrative parallel

    Both end the storm episode with the question of who Jesus is, prompted by the obedience of wind and sea. Matthew describes amazement; Mark emphasizes great fear.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 4:41 They were greatly afraid and said to one another, “Who then is this, that even the wind and the sea obey Him?”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:28

When He came to the other side, to the country of the Gadarenes, two men oppressed by demons met Him, coming out of the tombs. They were exceedingly fierce, so that no one could pass by that road.

  1. Mark 5:1–5:3 narrative parallel

    Both describe meeting demon-afflicted people associated with tombs after the crossing. Matthew names the Gadarenes and two men, while Mark names the Gerasenes and focuses on one man; the place-name and participant-count differences remain explicit.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 5:1 They came to the other side of the sea, to the country of the Gerasenes.

    Mark 5:2 As He stepped out of the boat, a man with an unclean spirit immediately met Him from the tombs.

    Mark 5:3 He made his dwelling among the tombs, and no one could bind him anymore, even with a chain.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:29

And behold, they cried out, “What have we to do with Thee, Son of God? Hast Thou come here to torment us before the appointed time?”

  1. Mark 5:7 narrative parallel

    Both contain a cry asking what Jesus, identified as God's Son, has to do with the afflicted speaker and an appeal concerning torment. Matthew has plural speakers and adds before the appointed time; Mark reports one man's cry and says Son of the Most High God.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 5:7 He cried out with a loud voice and said, “What hast Thou to do with me, Jesus, Son of God Most High? I adjure Thee by God: do not torment me!”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 8:30

Far from them a herd of many pigs was feeding.

  1. Mark 5:11 narrative parallel

    Both identify a large herd of pigs feeding near the exorcism scene. Matthew describes the herd as far from the men, while Mark places it there near the mountain.

    Read matching passage

Matthew 8:31

The demons pleaded with Him, saying, “If Thou castest us out, send us into the herd of pigs.”

    Both record the demons asking Jesus to send them into the pigs. Mark's narration focuses on one afflicted man, unlike Matthew's two men.

Matthew 8:32

He said to them, “Go.” They came out and went into the pigs. And behold, the whole herd rushed down the steep bank into the sea and died in the waters.

    Both narrate Jesus permitting the departure, the spirits entering the pigs, and the herd rushing down the bank and drowning in the sea. Mark adds an approximate herd count; Matthew gives none.

The herdsmen fled, and going into the city they reported everything, including what had happened to the men oppressed by demons.

And behold, the whole city came out to meet Jesus. When they saw Him, they begged Him to depart from their territory.

  1. Mark 5:17 narrative parallel

    Both conclude the encounter with local people asking Jesus to leave their territory. Matthew describes the whole city coming to meet Him, whereas the cited Mark verse gives the request without that collective description.

    Both introduce the paralytic-healing setting with Jesus returning to His home setting. Mark explicitly names Capernaum; Matthew calls it His own city after the lake crossing and does not name the city in this verse.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:1 When He entered Capernaum again after some days, it was heard that He was at home.

Matthew 9:2

And behold, they brought Him a paralytic lying on a bed. Seeing their faith, Jesus said to the paralytic, “Take courage, child; your sins are forgiven.”

  1. Mark 2:3–2:5 narrative parallel

    Both describe a paralytic being brought to Jesus, Jesus seeing their faith, and His declaration that the man's sins are forgiven. Mark adds four bearers and the opening in the roof; Matthew gives a shorter account.

    Mark 2:5 Jesus saw their faith and said to the paralyzed man, “Child, your sins are forgiven.”

Matthew 9:3

And behold, some of the scribes said among themselves, “This Man blasphemes.”

    Both describe scribes responding to Jesus' forgiveness declaration by regarding it as blasphemy. Mark further states their objection that only God can forgive sins.

Matthew 9:4

Seeing their thoughts, Jesus said, “Why do you think evil things in your hearts?

  1. Mark 2:8 narrative parallel

    Both have Jesus perceive the scribes' inward reasoning and challenge what they are thinking in their hearts. Matthew calls the thoughts evil; Mark explicitly says He perceived them in His spirit.

    Read matching passage

Matthew 9:5

For which is easier, to say, ‘Your sins are forgiven,’ or to say, ‘Rise and walk’?

  1. Mark 2:9 narrative parallel

    Both contrast the ease of saying sins are forgiven with telling the paralytic to rise and walk. Mark includes taking up the bed within the second alternative.

But so that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins”—then He said to the paralytic—“rise, take your bed, and go to your house.”

  1. Mark 2:10–2:11 narrative parallel

    Both connect the healing command with demonstration that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins, then direct the paralytic to rise, take his bed and go home.

When the crowds saw this, they feared and glorified God, who had given such authority to human beings.

  1. Mark 2:12 narrative parallel

    Read matching passage

Matthew 9:9

As Jesus passed on from there, He saw a man called Matthew sitting at the tax station, and He said to him, “Follow Me.” He rose and followed Him.

  1. Mark 2:14 narrative parallel

    Both narrate Jesus calling a man seated at a tax station with Follow Me, and the man rising to follow. Matthew names him Matthew; Mark names him Levi son of Alphaeus. The cross-reference compares the narratives without resolving the different names.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:14 As He passed by, He saw Levi son of Alphaeus sitting at the tax station. He said to him, “Follow Me.” He arose and followed Him.

Matthew 9:10

  1. Mark 2:15 narrative parallel

    Both describe Jesus and His disciples reclining at table among many tax collectors and sinners. The comparison concerns the shared meal setting without resolving the Matthew/Levi naming difference in the adjoining call accounts.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:15 As He reclined at table in his house, many tax collectors and sinners reclined with Jesus and His disciples, for there were many who followed Him.

Matthew 9:11

When the Pharisees saw this, they said to His disciples, “Why does your Teacher eat with tax collectors and sinners?”

  1. Mark 2:16 narrative parallel

    Both record religious critics asking the disciples why Jesus eats with tax collectors and sinners. Matthew names Pharisees; Mark specifies the scribes of the Pharisees.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:16 The scribes of the Pharisees saw that He was eating with sinners and tax collectors and said to His disciples, “Why does He eat with tax collectors and sinners?”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 9:12

Hearing this, He said, “The strong have no need of a physician, but those who are ill do.

  1. Mark 2:17 narrative parallel

    Both answer the criticism with the physician analogy: the strong or healthy do not need a physician, but the sick do. Mark continues with the call of sinners within the same verse.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:17 Jesus heard and said to them, “The strong have no need of a physician, but those who are ill do. I did not come to call the righteous, but sinners.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 9:13

Go and learn what this means: ‘I desire mercy and not sacrifice.’ For I did not come to call righteous people, but sinners.”

  1. Hosea 6:6 quotation

    Jesus explicitly cites the preference for mercy rather than sacrifice. Hosea supplies that wording and continues with knowledge of God rather than whole burnt offerings; it does not itself contain Matthew's following statement about calling sinners.

    Read matching passage

    Hosea 6:6 For I desire mercy, and not sacrifice, and knowledge of God rather than whole burnt offerings.

Matthew 9:14

  1. Mark 2:18 narrative parallel

    Both ask why John's disciples and the Pharisees fast while Jesus' disciples do not. Matthew makes John's disciples the questioners; Mark introduces the practice and reports people putting the question.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:18 John’s disciples and the Pharisees were fasting. They came and said to Him, “Why do John’s disciples and the disciples of the Pharisees fast, but Thy disciples do not fast?”

  1. Mark 2:19–2:20 narrative parallel

    Both use the bridegroom's presence to explain why the wedding party does not fast and predict fasting when he is taken away. Matthew's opening question speaks of mourning; Mark's speaks directly of fasting.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:19 Jesus said to them, “Can the bridegroom’s companions fast while the bridegroom is with them? As long as they have the bridegroom with them, they cannot fast.

Matthew 9:16

No one puts a patch of unshrunk cloth on an old garment, for its patch pulls away from the garment, and a worse tear results.

  1. Mark 2:21 teaching parallel

    Both illustrate incompatible old and new with an unshrunk patch pulling away from an old garment and making the tear worse.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:21 No one sews a patch of unshrunk cloth onto an old garment. Otherwise the new patch pulls away from the old, and a worse tear results.

Matthew 9:17

Nor do people put new wine into old wineskins. Otherwise the skins burst, the wine spills out, and the skins are ruined. Instead, they put new wine into fresh wineskins, and both are preserved.”

  1. Mark 2:22 teaching parallel

    Both explain that new wine bursts old wineskins and destroys wine and skins, then require fresh skins for new wine. Matthew adds that both are preserved.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:22 No one puts new wine into old wineskins. Otherwise the wine will burst the skins, and the wine is lost along with the skins. New wine belongs in fresh wineskins.”

While He was saying these things to them, behold, a ruler came and bowed before Him, saying, “My daughter has just died. But come, lay Thy hand upon her, and she will live.”

  1. Mark 5:22–5:23 narrative parallel

    Both have a ruler approach Jesus and ask Him to lay hands on his daughter so she may live. Mark names Jairus and describes her as near death at this point; Matthew reports her as already just dead. The initial conditions differ rather than being silently merged.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 5:22 One of the synagogue rulers came, named Jairus. Seeing Him, he fell at His feet

Matthew 9:25

  1. Mark 5:40–5:42 narrative parallel

    Both have the outsiders removed, Jesus take the girl's hand, and the girl rise. Mark preserves His spoken command and her age and walking; Matthew abbreviates those details.

    Read matching passage

Matthew 9:26

This report went out into all that land.

  1. Luke 7:14–7:17 thematic parallel: reports following restoration of the dead

    Both narratives describe a report spreading regionally after the restoration of a dead person. Matthew closes the raising of the girl with a report throughout the land; Luke describes the young man being raised and the report spreading through Judea and the surrounding region. These are corresponding narrative effects in different episodes, not the same incident.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:14 He approached and touched the bier; the bearers stood still. He said, “Young man, I tell you, rise.”

    Luke 7:15 The dead man sat up and began speaking, and He gave him to his mother.

    Luke 7:16 Fear seized everyone, and they glorified God, saying, “A great prophet has arisen among us,” and, “God has visited His people.”

    Luke 7:17 This report about Him spread throughout all Judea and all the surrounding region.

Matthew 9:27

As Jesus passed on from there, two blind men followed Him, crying out, “Have mercy on us, Son of David!”

  1. Matthew 20:30–20:31 narrative parallel

    A later, distinct healing encounter likewise involves two blind men crying for mercy and addressing Jesus as Son of David. Matthew 9 places its approach before entering a house, whereas Matthew 20 places the men beside the road at Jericho; the events are not identified as one.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 20:30 And behold, two blind men sitting beside the road heard that Jesus was passing by and cried out, “Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David!”

    Matthew 20:31 The crowd rebuked them to make them silent, but they cried out all the more, “Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David!”

  1. Mark 9:23–9:24 thematic parallel

    Both healing settings explicitly raise believing in Jesus' ability: Jesus asks the blind men whether they believe He can act, while in Mark He answers a father's if Thou canst and the father professes faith with a plea about unbelief. The sufferers and exchanges are different.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 9:23 Jesus said to him, “‘If Thou canst’! All things are possible for the one who believes.”

    Mark 9:24 Immediately the father of the child cried out and said, “I believe; help my unbelief!”

Matthew 9:29

  1. Matthew 20:34 narrative parallel

    Both distinct blind-healing episodes describe Jesus touching the men's eyes. The later Jericho account reports compassion and immediate sight; this verse relates the outcome to faith. Shared action does not make the occasions identical.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 20:34 Moved with compassion, Jesus touched their eyes. Immediately they received sight and followed Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 9:30

Their eyes were opened. Jesus sternly warned them, saying, “See that no one knows.”

  1. Matthew 20:34 narrative parallel

    Both distinct encounters conclude with the blind men's eyes receiving sight. The Jericho verse does not include Matthew 9's command to keep the healing unknown, and no secrecy instruction is inferred for that later event.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 20:34 Moved with compassion, Jesus touched their eyes. Immediately they received sight and followed Him.

    Matthew's instructions restrict this mission away from Gentile routes and Samaritan cities. Acts later explicitly commissions witness in Samaria and to the earth's end. This is a contrast between commissions in different narrative periods, not an assertion that their geographical instructions are identical.

    Both restrict mission equipment and disallow two tunics. Matthew also forbids sandals and a staff, whereas Mark permits a staff and instructs wearing sandals; this real equipment difference is retained.

    Both use the relationship between master and subordinate to explain why disciples can expect treatment like Jesus'. John makes persecution explicit; Matthew's example is the household master's being called Beelzebul, a detail absent from the cited John verse.

  1. Luke 12:7 teaching parallel

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:32

Therefore everyone who acknowledges Me before people, I too will acknowledge before My Father in the heavens.

  1. Luke 12:8 teaching parallel

    Both connect acknowledging Jesus before people with a corresponding heavenly acknowledgment. Matthew promises acknowledgment before His Father; Luke describes the Son of Man's acknowledgment before God's angels.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 12:8 And I tell you, everyone who acknowledges Me before people, the Son of Man will also acknowledge before the angels of God.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:33

But whoever denies Me before people, I too will deny before My Father in the heavens.

  1. Luke 12:9 teaching parallel

    Both warn that denying Jesus before people leads to heavenly denial. Matthew names His Father as the heavenly audience, while Luke names God's angels.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 12:9 But whoever denies Me before people will be denied before the angels of God.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:34

Do not suppose that I came to bring peace upon the earth. I did not come to bring peace, but a sword.

  1. Luke 12:51 teaching parallel

    Both challenge the expectation that Jesus' coming brings uncomplicated earthly peace. Matthew uses the image of a sword; Luke explicitly says division.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 12:51 Do you think I have come to give peace on earth? No, I tell you, but division.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:35

For I came to divide a man against his father, a daughter against her mother, and a daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law,

  1. Luke 12:53 teaching parallel

    Both describe divisions involving father and son, mother and daughter, and mother-in-law and daughter-in-law. Luke gives reciprocal formulations, while Matthew states the divisions in one direction.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 12:53 They will be divided: father against son and son against father, mother against daughter and daughter against mother, mother-in-law against her daughter-in-law and daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law.”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:36

and a person’s enemies will be the members of his own household.

  1. Micah 7:6 allusion

    Matthew's statement that a person's enemies will be members of the household recalls Micah's concluding statement that a man's enemies are the men in his house. Micah also lists family divisions matching the preceding Matthew verse.

    Read matching passage

    Micah 7:6 For a son dishonors his father, a daughter will rise against her mother, a daughter-in-law will rise against her mother-in-law; a man’s enemies are all the men in his house.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:37

Whoever loves father or mother more than Me is not worthy of Me; and whoever loves son or daughter more than Me is not worthy of Me.

  1. Luke 14:26 teaching parallel

    Both demand allegiance to Jesus over the closest family relationships. Matthew expresses this as not loving parents or children more than Him; Luke uses the stronger language of hating family and one's own life. The wordings are not harmonized into an exact quotation.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 14:26 “If anyone comes to Me and does not hate his own father and mother and wife and children and brothers and sisters, and even his own life, he cannot be My disciple.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:38

Whoever does not take his cross and follow after Me is not worthy of Me.

  1. Luke 14:27 teaching parallel

    Both make taking or carrying one's cross and coming after Jesus a requirement of discipleship. Matthew describes the person as not worthy of Him, while Luke says the person cannot be His disciple.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 14:27 Whoever does not carry his own cross and come after Me cannot be My disciple.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:39

Whoever finds his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life because of Me will find it.

  1. Mark 8:35 teaching parallel

    Both reverse the attempt to preserve one's life: losing it because of Jesus leads to gaining or saving it. Mark explicitly adds the gospel as a cause and uses save where Matthew uses find.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 8:35 For whoever wants to save his life will lose it; but whoever loses his life for My sake and for the gospel will save it.

Matthew 10:40

Whoever receives you receives Me, and whoever receives Me receives the One who sent Me.

    Both describe reception of Jesus' messenger as reception of Jesus and reception of Jesus as reception of the One who sent Him.

    Read matching passage

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:41

Whoever receives a prophet in the name of a prophet will receive a prophet’s reward, and whoever receives a righteous person in the name of a righteous person will receive a righteous person’s reward.

  1. 2 Kings 4:8–4:10 thematic parallel

    The Shunammite woman's hospitality to Elisha because she recognizes him as a holy man of God illustrates receiving a prophet in that capacity. These verses narrate bread and lodging; they do not themselves state Matthew's general promise of a prophet's reward.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 4:8 One day Elisha passed through Shunem. A prominent woman was there, and she pressed him to eat bread. As he continued coming there over time, he would turn aside to eat there.

    2 Kings 4:9 The woman said to her husband, ‘Behold, I know that this is a holy man of God who continually passes by us.’

    2 Kings 4:10 ‘Please let us make a small upstairs place for him and put a bed, a table, a stool, and a lampstand there for him. When he comes to us, he can turn aside there.’

    Pinned target record

Matthew 10:42

And whoever gives one of these little ones even a cup of cold water in the name of a disciple, truly I tell you, will certainly not lose his reward.”

  1. Mark 9:41 teaching parallel

    Both promise that giving a cup of water on account of the recipient's belonging to Jesus will not lose its reward. Matthew says cold water to one of these little ones in a disciple's name; Mark speaks of giving to the disciples because they are Christ's.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 9:41 Whoever gives you a cup of water to drink in recognition that you belong to Christ, truly I tell you, will certainly not lose his reward.

Matthew 11:1

  1. Matthew 13:53 same-book structural parallel: completed teaching and departure

    Both notices use the completion of Jesus' teaching to introduce His moving on. One follows the directions to the Twelve and leads to teaching and proclaiming in their cities; the other follows the parables. This compares an explicit internal transition pattern without identifying the journeys as one occasion.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 13:53 When Jesus finished these parables, He departed from there.

Matthew 11:5

the blind receive sight and the lame walk, lepers are cleansed and the deaf hear, the dead are raised and the poor have good news proclaimed to them.

Matthew 11:10

This is the one about whom it is written: ‘Behold, I send My messenger before Thy face, who will prepare Thy way before Thee.’

  1. Malachi 3:1 quotation

    Matthew's messenger preparing the way is grounded in Malachi's announced messenger. The Saints Malachi wording has the way before My face and goes on to the Lord's coming to His sanctuary, whereas Matthew addresses Thy face and Thy way; the differences remain visible.

    Read matching passage

    Malachi 3:1 “Look, I am sending out My messenger, and he will look to a way before My face. Suddenly the Lord whom you seek will come to His own sanctuary, and the messenger of the covenant whom you desire. Look, he is coming,” says the Lord Almighty.

Matthew 11:12

From the days of John the Baptist until now, the kingdom of the heavens suffers violence, and violent men seize it.

  1. Luke 16:16 teaching parallel

    Both link John with a new phase of the kingdom and use language of force or pressing. Matthew says the kingdom suffers violence and violent men seize it; Luke says the kingdom is proclaimed and everyone presses into it. The comparison does not resolve these distinct expressions into one interpretation.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 16:16 The Law and the Prophets were until John. From that time the kingdom of God is proclaimed as good news, and everyone presses into it.

Matthew 11:13

For all the Prophets and the Law prophesied until John.

  1. Luke 16:16 teaching parallel

    Both place the Law and Prophets up to John. Luke continues with the proclamation of God's kingdom from then onward, while Matthew explicitly says the Law and Prophets prophesied until John.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 16:16 The Law and the Prophets were until John. From that time the kingdom of God is proclaimed as good news, and everyone presses into it.

Matthew 11:14

And if you are willing to receive it, he is Elijah who is to come.

  1. Luke 1:17 thematic parallel

    Matthew identifies John with the Elijah who is to come; Luke describes John's going before the Lord in Elijah's spirit and power. This connects the Elijah role without claiming John and the historical Elijah are literally the same person.

    Read matching passage

  1. Luke 7:34–7:35 teaching parallel

    Read matching passage

    Luke 10:13 Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty deeds performed among you had happened in Tyre and Sidon, they would long ago have repented, sitting in sackcloth and ashes.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 11:21

“Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty works that occurred in you had occurred in Tyre and Sidon, they would long ago have repented in sackcloth and ashes.

  1. Luke 10:13 teaching parallel

    Both pronounce woe on Chorazin and Bethsaida and say Tyre and Sidon would have repented in sackcloth and ashes if they had experienced the same mighty works.

    Read matching passage

Matthew 11:23

    Both address Capernaum with the contrast between supposed exaltation to heaven and descent to Hades. Luke's verse does not include Matthew's additional comparison with Sodom's survival.

    Read matching passage

Matthew 11:25

Matthew 11:28

Come to Me, all you who labour and are burdened, and I will give you rest.

  1. Sirach 51:23–51:27 thematic parallel

    Both present an invitation associated with a teacher, instruction and rest. Sirach invites the uninstructed near, speaks of a yoke and reports finding much rest after little labor; Jesus invites the burdened to Himself and promises to give rest. This is a wisdom-teaching comparison, not a claim that Sirach names Jesus.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 51:23 Draw near to me, you who are uninstructed, and lodge in a house of instruction.

    Sirach 51:24 Why do you say you are lacking in these things, while your souls thirst exceedingly?

    Sirach 51:25 I opened my mouth and said, “Acquire for yourselves without silver.

    Sirach 51:26 “Put your neck beneath the yoke, and let your soul receive instruction. She is near at hand to be found.

    Sirach 51:27 “See with your own eyes that I labored a little and found much rest for myself.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 11:29

Take My yoke upon you, and learn from Me; for I am meek and lowly in heart, and you shall find rest for your souls.

  1. Sirach 51:26–51:27 allusion

    The combination of taking a yoke, receiving instruction and finding rest recalls Sirach's invitation to put the neck beneath the yoke and receive instruction, followed by much rest. Matthew centers the invitation on learning from Jesus and adds His meekness and humility.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 51:26 “Put your neck beneath the yoke, and let your soul receive instruction. She is near at hand to be found.

    Sirach 51:27 “See with your own eyes that I labored a little and found much rest for myself.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 11:30

  1. Sirach 51:27 thematic parallel

    Jesus describes His yoke as easy and burden as light, while Sirach describes little labor yielding much rest. The comparison concerns the rewarding lightness of instruction; Sirach does not repeat Matthew's statement about Jesus' own yoke.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 51:27 “See with your own eyes that I labored a little and found much rest for myself.

Matthew 12:3

Matthew 12:4

how he entered the house of God, and they ate the bread of the Presence, which it was not lawful for him or those with him to eat, but only for the priests?

  1. 1 Samuel 21:5–21:7 allusion

    Samuel narrates the priest giving David holy loaves of presentation, the bread of the Presence, after discussion about the young men's purity. This supplies the bread episode Jesus recalls; these cited verses do not themselves state Matthew's full legal restriction to priests.

    Read matching passage

    1 Samuel 21:5 The priest answered David and said, “There are no common loaves at hand, only holy loaves. If the young men have kept themselves from a woman, then he may eat.”

    1 Samuel 21:6 David answered the priest and said to him, “But we have abstained from a woman yesterday and the third day. When I went out on the road, all the young men had been purified. The road itself is common, for it will be sanctified today through my equipment.”

    1 Samuel 21:7 Abimelech the priest gave him the loaves of presentation, for there was no bread there except the loaves of the Presence, removed from before the Lord so that hot bread could be set out on the day he took them.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 12:5

Or have you not read in the Law that on Sabbaths the priests in the temple profane the Sabbath and are guiltless?

  1. Numbers 28:9–28:10 allusion

    The law prescribes additional burnt offerings on each Sabbath, supplying the commanded temple-service background for Jesus' argument about priests working on Sabbaths without guilt. Numbers states the offering requirement; it does not itself call the service profaning the Sabbath.

    Read matching passage

    Numbers 28:9 And on the Sabbath day you shall bring two unblemished year-old lambs, and two tenths of fine flour mixed with oil for a sacrifice, and a drink offering,

    Numbers 28:10 a Sabbath whole burnt offering on each Sabbath, besides the continual whole burning and its drink offering.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 12:6

But I tell you that something greater than the temple is here.

  1. John 2:19–2:21 thematic parallel

    Matthew claims something greater than the temple is present. John explains Jesus' temple saying as referring to the sanctuary of His body. These are distinct ways of relating Jesus to the sanctuary; John does not repeat Matthew's greater-than comparison.

    Read matching passage

    John 2:19 Jesus answered them, “Destroy this sanctuary, and in three days I will raise it.”

    John 2:20 So the Jews said, “This sanctuary was built in forty-six years, and wilt Thou raise it in three days?”

    John 2:21 But He was speaking about the sanctuary of His body.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 12:7

If you had known what ‘I desire mercy and not sacrifice’ means, you would not have condemned the guiltless.

  1. Hosea 6:6 quotation

    Jesus' mercy-not-sacrifice quotation matches Hosea's statement of the Lord's desire for mercy rather than sacrifice. Matthew applies it to the condemnation of the guiltless in the Sabbath dispute; Hosea continues with knowledge of God over burnt offerings.

    Read matching passage

    Hosea 6:6 For I desire mercy, and not sacrifice, and knowledge of God rather than whole burnt offerings.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 12:8

For the Son of Man is Lord of the Sabbath.”

  1. Mark 2:28 narrative parallel

    Both conclude the grainfield controversy with the Son of Man's lordship over the Sabbath.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:28 So the Son of Man is Lord even of the Sabbath.”

Matthew 12:9

  1. Mark 3:1 narrative parallel

    Both move into a synagogue scene that introduces a man with a withered hand. Matthew's verse gives the entry, while Mark includes the man's presence in the same verse.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 3:1 He entered a synagogue again, and a man with a withered hand was there.

Matthew 12:10

And behold, a man had a withered hand. They asked Him, “Is it lawful to heal on Sabbaths?” so that they might accuse Him.

  1. Luke 6:7 narrative parallel

    Both place Sabbath healing under hostile scrutiny aimed at finding an accusation. Matthew reports a direct question about lawfulness, while Luke describes the scribes and Pharisees watching whether Jesus would heal.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:7 The scribes and Pharisees watched closely to see whether He would heal on the Sabbath, so that they might find a way to accuse Him.

Matthew 12:11

He said to them, “Who among you, if he has one sheep and it falls into a pit on a Sabbath, will not grasp it and lift it out?

  1. Luke 14:5 teaching parallel

    Both appeal to rescuing a vulnerable creature from a pit or well on the Sabbath as an answer to healing objections. Matthew uses a sheep and its being grasped and lifted; Luke's supplied saying uses a son or an ox, so the examples and narrative occasions differ.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 14:5 And He said to them, “Which of you, if his son or ox falls into a well, will not pull him out at once on the Sabbath day?”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 12:12

How much more valuable, then, is a human being than a sheep! So it is lawful to do good on Sabbaths.”

  1. Luke 6:9 teaching parallel

    Both argue the Sabbath question in terms of doing good. Matthew concludes it is lawful to do good and compares a human's value with a sheep's; Luke asks whether doing good or evil, saving life or destroying it, is lawful.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:9 Jesus said to them, “I ask you: Is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or do evil, to save a life or destroy it?”

    Pinned target record

Matthew 12:13

Then He said to the man, “Stretch out your hand.” He stretched it out, and it was restored, healthy like the other.

  1. Mark 3:5 narrative parallel

    Both give Jesus' command to stretch out the hand, the man's action and its restoration. Matthew explicitly says it became healthy like the other; Mark describes Jesus' anger and grief before the command.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 3:5 He looked around at them with anger, grieving at the hardness of their hearts, and said to the man, “Stretch out your hand.” He stretched it out, and his hand was restored.

Matthew 12:14

The Pharisees went out and took counsel against Him, how they might destroy Him.

  1. Mark 3:6 narrative parallel

    Both have the Pharisees go out and take counsel about destroying Jesus after the healing. Mark additionally names the Herodians as partners in the plot.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 3:6 The Pharisees went out and immediately took counsel with the Herodians against Him, as to how they might destroy Him.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 12:15

But Jesus, knowing this, withdrew from there. Great crowds followed Him, and He healed them all.

  1. Mark 3:7–3:10 narrative parallel

    Both describe withdrawal, crowds following and healing activity. Matthew says He healed them all and links withdrawal to knowledge of the plot; Mark lists the crowds' regions and says He healed many, so scope and wording are not made identical.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 3:7 Jesus withdrew with His disciples to the sea, and a great multitude from Galilee followed; and from Judea,

    Mark 3:10 For He healed many, so that all who had afflictions pressed upon Him to touch Him.

He sternly warned them not to make Him known,

  1. Mark 3:12 narrative parallel

    Both report a stern command not to make Jesus known. Matthew addresses the healed crowd in the surrounding narrative; Mark's immediate recipients are the unclean spirits, a difference in audience retained here.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 3:12 He sternly warned them not to make Him known.

Matthew 12:17

so that what was spoken through Isaiah the prophet might be fulfilled:

  1. Isaiah 42:1–42:4 quotation

    Matthew introduces the following servant passage as fulfillment of Isaiah. The cited Saints Isaiah unit supplies the chosen servant, Spirit, restrained public conduct, bruised reed and nations' hope, though its naming and some wording differ from Matthew's quotation.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 42:1 “Jacob is My servant; I will uphold him. Israel is My chosen one; My soul accepted him. I put My spirit upon him; he will bring judgment to the nations.

    Isaiah 42:2 He will not cry out or raise his voice, nor will his voice be heard outside.

    Isaiah 42:3 He will not crush a bruised reed or extinguish a smoking flax wick; rather, he will bring judgment in truth.

    Isaiah 42:4 He will shine forth and will not be broken until he sets judgment upon the earth, and nations will hope in his name.”

Matthew 12:18

“Behold, My Servant whom I chose, My beloved in whom My soul was well pleased. I will put My Spirit upon Him, and He will proclaim justice to the nations.

  1. Isaiah 42:1 quotation

    Both describe God's chosen servant, divine approval, the Spirit placed upon him and judgment proclaimed to the nations. Saints Isaiah explicitly names Jacob as servant and Israel as chosen one, whereas Matthew has My Servant and My beloved; this difference and Matthew's application to Jesus remain explicit.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 42:1 “Jacob is My servant; I will uphold him. Israel is My chosen one; My soul accepted him. I put My spirit upon him; he will bring judgment to the nations.

    Both contrast the forgiveness available for sins and blasphemies with the exception of blasphemy against the Holy Spirit. Mark states the exclusion in terms of never having forgiveness and liability for an eternal sin; Matthew's cited verse does not use that final phrase.

    Matthew's righteous shining like the sun recalls Daniel's understanding people appearing like heaven's lights and those strengthening his words like stars. This is an illuminating-light comparison, with Daniel's supplied Greek-based wording retained rather than replaced by another edition.

  1. Matthew 19:21 thematic parallel

    The parable's man sells his possessions for the treasure-bearing field; Jesus elsewhere calls the rich man to sell his belongings, give to the poor, receive heavenly treasure, and follow Him. The connection is costly pursuit of kingdom treasure, without equating the two transactions.

    Both sentences say that Jesus finished a block of teaching and then departed. Matthew 13 closes the parables; Matthew 19 closes the preceding words and names the move from Galilee toward Judea beyond the Jordan. The shared discourse-closing and departure pattern is an internal literary connection, not independent testimony or an identical journey.

    Matthew 7:9 Or who among you, if his son asks for bread, will give him a stone?

  1. Matthew 5:33–5:35 thematic parallel

    Jesus' earlier teaching rejects oath-taking by sacred or divine-associated objects rather than establishing classes of supposedly binding and nonbinding oaths. This same-book comparison gives the broader oath-teaching background; the earlier passage does not specifically discuss sanctuary gold.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 5:33 Again, you have heard that it was said to those of old: ‘You shall not swear falsely, but shall fulfil your oaths to the Lord.’

    Matthew 5:34 But I tell you: do not swear at all, neither by heaven, for it is the throne of God,

    Matthew 5:35 nor by the earth, for it is the footstool of His feet, nor by Jerusalem, for it is the city of the great King.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 23:18

And, ‘Whoever swears by the altar, it is nothing; but whoever swears by the gift upon it is bound.’

  1. Matthew 5:34–5:35 thematic parallel

    The earlier same-book instruction not to swear at all bears on the disputed distinction between swearing by an altar and swearing by its gift. Its examples are heaven, earth and Jerusalem, not this verse's altar and gift, so it supplies a related principle rather than an exact restatement.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 5:34 But I tell you: do not swear at all, neither by heaven, for it is the throne of God,

    Matthew 5:35 nor by the earth, for it is the footstool of His feet, nor by Jerusalem, for it is the city of the great King.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 23:19

Blind men! For which is greater, the gift or the altar that sanctifies the gift?

  1. Exodus 29:37 allusion

    Both attribute sanctifying holiness to the altar. Exodus calls the altar most holy and says everyone touching it will be sanctified, while Matthew specifically speaks of the altar sanctifying a gift. The persons-and-gift difference and the absence of an oath rule in Exodus are retained.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 29:37 For seven days you shall purify the altar and consecrate it; and the altar shall be most holy; everyone who touches the altar shall be sanctified.

    Pinned target record

Matthew 23:21

The one swearing by the sanctuary swears by it and by the One dwelling in it.

  1. Psalms 10:4 thematic parallel

    Matthew relates the sanctuary to the One dwelling in it; the psalm says the Lord is in His holy temple and immediately places His throne in heaven. The comparison concerns sacred dwelling and divine presence, without claiming the psalm identifies the same physical sanctuary.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 10:4 The Lord is in His holy temple; the Lord’s throne is in heaven. His eyes look toward the needy; His eyelids examine the sons of men.

Matthew 23:22

The one swearing by heaven swears by God’s throne and by the One seated upon it.

  1. Isaiah 66:1 allusion

    Matthew's identification of heaven as God's throne recalls the Lord's statement that heaven is His throne. Matthew applies that imagery to swearing by heaven; Isaiah is not itself an oath prohibition.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 66:1 Thus says the Lord: “Heaven is My throne, and earth the footstool of My feet. What sort of house will you build for Me, or what place is My rest?

  1. Luke 11:42 teaching parallel

    Both rebuke meticulous tithing of herbs while neglecting weightier duties, and say the latter should be done without omitting the former. Matthew names mint, dill and cumin with justice, mercy and faithfulness; Luke names mint, rue and every herb with justice and love of God.

Blind Pharisee, first cleanse the inside of the cup, so that its outside may also become clean.

  1. Luke 11:39–11:41 teaching parallel

    Both direct attention from the cleaned exterior to the morally disordered interior. Matthew commands cleansing the inside first so the outside becomes clean; Luke connects inner and outer creation and instructs giving what is within as alms. The remedies are compared without being made identical.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 11:40 Fools! Did not the One who made the outside also make the inside?

    Luke 11:41 But give what is within as alms, and behold, everything is clean for you.

    Both transitions begin by saying that Jesus finished these words.     Both accounts relate Judas's death in connection with betrayal money, but the details differ: Matthew says he returned the coins and hanged himself; Acts describes a field acquired from wrongdoing wages and a headlong fall with bodily rupture. No combined death sequence is invented.

Reviewed units still unfinished

  • Matthew 1:14: The actual Matthew lineage was reread beside Luke 3:23–24 and the postexilic families in 1 Chronicles 3:19–24. Those records do not establish this Azor–Zadok–Achim–Eliud chain; similarly situated or repeated names cannot settle these identities. No pair is retained.
  • Matthew 1:15: The Matthew chain was reconsidered with Luke 3:23–24 and 1 Chronicles 3:19–24. Neither supplied passage attests the Eliud–Eleazar–Matthan–Jacob chain. No identity or relationship is manufactured from similar names.
  • Matthew 3:15: Matthew 5:17 and 21:32 were reread with the baptismal exchange. They concern fulfillment and John's righteous way, but do not explain Jesus permitting this baptism to fulfill all righteousness. The distinctive exchange remains without a retained counterpart.
  • Matthew 6:3: The almsgiving instruction and its secrecy image were reconsidered in Matthew 6:1–4. The surrounding reward language does not independently reproduce the left-hand/right-hand image, so repeating neighboring context would not resolve this deferred unit.
  • Matthew 6:17: The grooming instructions were reconsidered beside 2 Samuel 12:20 and its fasting context. David washes and anoints after his fast; Matthew commands ordinary appearance while fasting. That limited overlap does not establish a close counterpart to this particular fasting instruction.
  • Matthew 8:1: Luke 6:17 was reread as a candidate because it describes descent and a crowd. Its distinct level-place scene does not supply a sufficiently specific counterpart to Matthew's transition after the mountain teaching. No identity of occasion or completion is claimed.
  • Matthew 13:29: The warning about uprooting wheat while gathering darnel was reread with the later explanation in Matthew 13:37–43. The explanation identifies the plants and harvest but not this hazard of premature removal. The warning remains deferred.
  • Matthew 13:52: The scribe-householder comparison was reread with the surrounding parable conclusions. A generic overlap involving treasure or old/new possessions does not establish its particular claim about a scribe made a kingdom disciple. No unsupported allusion is retained.
  • Matthew 14:18: Mark 6:38–39 was reread with the feeding context. It records counting the loaves and arranging the people, without Jesus' particular command to bring the food to Him. Shared feeding context alone does not resolve this individual unit.
  • Matthew 14:28: Peter's request was reconsidered beside John 21:4–7. The later shore scene has recognition of the Lord, but no corresponding request to be commanded onto the waters. The episodes and the distinctive request are not conflated.
  • Matthew 14:29: John 21:4–7 was reread as a possible Peter-and-sea comparison. It describes Peter throwing himself into the sea after hearing that it is the Lord, not walking on it at Jesus' command. The limited shared setting was not retained as a sufficiently precise counterpart to this action.
  • Matthew 15:12: The offense report was reread with Mark 7:17 and its discourse context. Mark records the disciples asking in the house, not their report of the Pharisees' offense. A generic shared response to teaching does not resolve the stated report.
  • Matthew 15:23: Mark 7:24–30 was reread in full beside the woman's approach. It does not reproduce Jesus' initial silence or the disciples' request to dismiss her. No correspondence for those distinctive actions is credited.
  • Matthew 17:24: Exodus 30:13–15 was reread. Its actual wording gives half a didrachma for the census contribution, while Matthew speaks of the didrachmas collectors. The texts alone do not establish the levy's identity or amount; the proposed equivalence remains unresolved.
  • Matthew 17:25: The question was reread with the full Matthew 17:24–27 tax exchange and Exodus 30:13–15. The census contribution does not supply Jesus' sons-versus-strangers analogy about earthly kings. General tax vocabulary is insufficient.
  • Matthew 17:26: The conclusion was reconsidered in the immediate royal-tax analogy. General spiritual-freedom sayings would not reproduce the specific sons-versus-strangers reasoning. No equivalence between those different arguments is retained.
  • Matthew 18:31: The fellow servants' grief and report were reconsidered in Matthew 18:23–35. Neighboring responses explain the narrative but do not provide a separate precise counterpart to the report. It remains deferred without duplicating generic grief/reporting notices.
  • Matthew 19:10: 1 Corinthians 7:1 and 7:8–9 were reread as candidates. Paul's qualified advice to the unmarried and widows does not reproduce the disciples' reaction to the divorce instruction. Their reaction is not silently converted into Paul's counsel.
  • Matthew 20:3: The third-hour hiring detail was reread in the full Matthew 20:1–16 hiring narrative. Its exact time and marketplace action lack an established independent counterpart; another round of the same story is not used to create completion.
  • Matthew 20:5: The sixth- and ninth-hour hiring rounds were reconsidered in Matthew 20:1–16. Repetition within this single hiring sequence does not independently establish a precise cross-reference for these time details.
  • Matthew 20:6: The eleventh-hour question was reread with the full hiring sequence. No precise counterpart to the time and all-day-idleness question is established; broad vineyard or work imagery earns no credit.
  • Matthew 20:7: The un-hired workers' explanation and invitation were reconsidered in the complete hiring narrative. The generic invitation to work does not supply a precise independent counterpart to their stated reason and response.
  • Matthew 22:11: The king's inspection was reread with Matthew 22:1–14. General clothing images do not establish what this wedding garment represents or independently reproduce the inspection. No allegorical identity is asserted.
  • Matthew 22:12: The garment question and speechless response were reconsidered in Matthew 22:1–14. Neither generic garment symbolism nor an unrelated silence establishes a precise counterpart to this exchange.
  • Matthew 22:14: 1 Peter 2:9 was reread as a called/chosen candidate. It identifies a chosen people called out of darkness, without Matthew's many/few distinction. Shared vocabulary alone was not credited, and no unavailable Revelation text was invented.
  • Matthew 23:2: The specific statement about scribes and Pharisees occupying Moses' chair was read. General instructions to heed legal authorities do not establish this particular chair or its attribution to these groups; no sufficiently precise target was retained.
  • Matthew 23:10: The prohibition on being called guides, grounded in the one Guide, the Christ, was read. Broad passages about teachers or shepherds were not treated as direct counterparts to this distinct instruction.
  • Matthew 23:15: The indictment of travel to make a proselyte and then making him twice a son of Gehenna was read. No sufficiently precise separate passage for this distinctive conversion-and-corruption claim was established.
  • Matthew 23:17: The sanctuary's sanctifying the gold and the resulting ranking of sanctuary over gold were read. General consecration rules were not retained as though they explicitly establish this particular relationship or the disputed oath distinction.
  • Matthew 23:20: The inference that swearing by the altar includes everything upon it was read. Related altar-holiness rules do not themselves state this oath's scope; no sufficiently specific independent target was retained.
  • Matthew 23:24: The distinctive gnat-straining and camel-swallowing image was read. Other images of disproportionate moral attention were considered too general to serve as a close separate counterpart in this initial review.
  • Matthew 24:12: The prediction that increasing lawlessness will make many people's love grow cold was read. Broader lists of wrongdoing or apostasy do not repeat this causal connection, and no sufficiently specific target was retained.
  • Matthew 25:2: The five-foolish/five-prudent division was read. Broad wise-and-foolish comparisons were considered too general to establish a sufficiently specific counterpart to this source unit's distinctive division.
  • Matthew 25:7: The virgins' rising and putting their lamps in order was read. General lamp or awakening passages were not retained merely for shared imagery; no sufficiently precise counterpart was established.
  • Matthew 25:8: The foolish virgins' request to share oil because their lamps are going out was read. No sufficiently close counterpart was retained, and no unsupported allegorical identity for the oil was supplied.
  • Matthew 25:9: The prudent virgins' refusal based on limited supply and instruction to buy oil were read. General buying, sharing, or individual-responsibility teachings were not treated as precise counterparts to this distinctive parable detail.
  • Matthew 25:33: The assignment of sheep to the right and goats to the left was read. Ezekiel's flock judgment supports the preceding separation image but does not specify this right-and-left arrangement; no separate sufficiently close counterpart was retained for this unit.
  • Matthew 25:37: The righteous people's question about when they saw and fed the hungry or gave the thirsty King drink was read. Repetition of the scene's immediately preceding list was not treated as sufficient new cross-reference evidence for this distinctive question.
  • Matthew 25:39: The question about when they visited the sick or imprisoned King was read. General prison-care instructions were not counted as reproducing this distinctive question about unrecognized service.
  • Matthew 25:44: The condemned people's question about when they saw the King in these needs and failed to serve was read. No sufficiently close independent counterpart was retained, and the scene's repeated need-list was not used merely to manufacture completion.
  • Matthew 27:36: The soldiers sitting and keeping watch at the crucifixion were read. Other Gospel notices of soldiers at the cross do not precisely attest this sitting-watch detail; no generic presence link was retained for completion.
  • Matthew 27:53: The holy people leaving tombs after Jesus' rising, entering the holy city, and appearing to many were read. No precise independent counterpart was retained; passages about Jesus' appearances or general resurrection order were not made to narrate these specific appearances.
  • Matthew 27:62: The next-day approach of chief priests and Pharisees to Pilate was read. The distinctive guard-request transition has no sufficiently precise independent counterpart retained in this batch.
  • Matthew 27:65: Pilate's reply about a guard and making the tomb secure was read. No sufficiently precise independent counterpart was retained; later guard appearances alone were not used as a second confirmation of these particular words.
  • Matthew 28:4: The guards shaking and becoming like dead men from fear of the angel were read. Accounts of women fearing tomb messengers involve different participants; no generic angel-fear link was retained solely for completion.
  • Matthew 28:14: The leaders' promise to persuade the governor and protect the guards was read. Acts 12:18–19 was considered as a contrasting guard-liability narrative, but not retained as sufficiently precise evidence for this particular promised intervention.
